is continuous, it implies Assumption
2
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{Y_{z}\mid X,U=(m_{1},m_{0})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X,U=(m_{1},m_{0})\}\quad\text{(by \eqref{link} and $M_{z}=m_{z}$)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X)\quad\text{(by \eqref{spia})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X,M_{z}=m_{z})\quad\text{(by \eqref{spia})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z,M_{z}}\mid X,M_{z}=m_{z})=\mathbb{E}(Y_{z}\mid X,M_{z}=m_{z})\quad\text{(by \eqref{link})}.

•

The
sequential ignorability

[
Imai et al., 2010
,
Tchetgen and Shpitser, 2012
]
assumes for any
z,z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\text{(i). }\big(Y_{z^{\prime},m},M_{z}\big)\perp\!\!\!\perp Z\mid X;

(S10)

\displaystyle\text{(ii). }Y_{z^{\prime},m}\perp\!\!\!\perp M\mid Z=z,X,

(S11)

which imply Assumption
2
. This is because for any
z\in\{0,1\}
and
(m_{1},m_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z}\mid X,M_{z}=m_{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X,Z=1-z,M_{z}=m_{z})\quad\text{(by \eqref{link}\text{ and }\eqref{newlabel:aa})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X,Z=1-z,M_{z}=m_{z},M=m_{1-z})\quad\text{(by \eqref{newlabel:aa2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X,Z=1-z,M_{z}=m_{z},M_{1-z}=m_{1-z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{Y_{z,m_{z}}\mid X,U=(m_{1},m_{0})\}\quad\text{(by \eqref{newlabel:aa})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{Y_{z,M_{z}}\mid X,U=(m_{1},m_{0})\}=\mathbb{E}\{Y_{z}\mid X,U=(m_{1},m_{0})\}.

•

Parast et al. [2016]
and
Wang et al. [2020]
consider the RCT setting where
Z
is randomly assigned and no covariates
X
are collected. The identification assumption of
Parast et al. [2016]
assumes

\displaystyle Y_{1}\mbox{$\perp\!\!\!\perp$}M_{0}\mid M_{1}\text{ and }Y_{0}\mbox{$\perp\!\!\!\perp$}M_{1}\mid M_{0}.

(S12)

The identification assumption of
Wang et al. [2020]
assumes

\displaystyle(Y_{1},M_{1})\mbox{$\perp\!\!\!\perp$}(Y_{0},M_{0}).

(S13)

Under either (
S12
) or (
S13
), one can see that
Y_{z}\mid M_{z}=m_{z}
and
Y_{z}\mid U=u
share the same distribution and thus
\mathbb{E}(Y_{z}\mid M_{z}=m_{z})=\mathbb{E}\{Y_{z}\mid U=(m_{1},m_{0})\}
, which verifies Assumption
2
.

S3
Verification of CATE as a perfectly localized functional

In (
1
), let
W=(X,Z)
,
D=X
, and
x_{0}
be a specific individual level of
X
. Let
\gamma^{*}(x,z)=\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X=x,Z=z)
and
k_{h}(x\mid x_{0})
be some regular kernel centered at
x_{0}
. Denote

m(w,y,\gamma^{*},k_{h})=k_{h}(x\mid x_{0})\{\gamma^{*}(x,1)-\gamma^{*}(x,0)\}.

We show that CATE with
X=x_{0}
, namely
\vartheta_{0}=\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}-Y_{0}\mid X=x_{0})
, can be represented through (
1
) and thus is a perfectly localized function. In particular, under the assumption of selection on observables, we have

\vartheta_{0}=\mathbb{E}\{\gamma^{*}(X,1)-\gamma^{*}(X,0)\mid X=x_{0}\}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}[k_{h}(X\mid x_{0})\{\gamma^{*}(X,1)-\gamma^{*}(X,0)\}]=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\vartheta_{h}
, which complies with (
1
).

S4
Computational complexity of numerical integrations

In this section, using the standard Gaussian kernel as an example, we analyze the computational complexity of the numerical integrations required for our proposed estimator in (
11
). All integrations can be computed with a computational complexity bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}r_{n})
(ignoring logarithmic factors), ensuring the numerical approximation errors are controlled at
o(n^{-1/2})
.

Here,
r_{n}
represents the maximum computational cost for evaluating a single
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
or
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
given specific inputs, after all nuisance function estimators have been trained. Specifically, we define

\displaystyle r_{n}=\max\left\{\sup_{(x,u)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{M}^{2}}\text{CT}\{\hat{e}_{u}(x)\},\sup_{(x,z,m)\in\mathbb{X}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{M}}\text{CT}\{\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)\}\right\},

(S14)

where
\text{CT}\{g(v)\}
denotes the computational time required to calculate the function output
g(v)
with a given input
v
. We assume that the number of parameters in the estimators
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
grows with the sample size
n
, implying that the computational complexity of calculating a single
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
or
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
can be viewed as a quantity of
n
.

In the following, we present three examples to illustrate the different orders of
r_{n}
when
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
are estimated using different statistical methods. For simplicity, we assume that all involved random variables are uniformly bounded by a constant.

Example 1

Suppose we estimate
f_{zm}(x)
through the Gaussian linear model such that
\hat{f}_{zm}(x)=\varphi\{m\mid\hat{\ell}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
z\end{pmatrix},\hat{\sigma}^{2}\},

where
\hat{\ell}
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
are estimated coefficients in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+1}
and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively, and
\varphi(\cdot\mid\iota,\omega)
denotes the Gaussian density function with mean
\iota
and variance
\omega
. Then for any given
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
u\in\mathbb{M}^{2}
, all
\hat{f}_{1m_{1}}(x),\hat{f}_{0m_{0}}(x),\hat{F}_{1m_{1}}(x),\hat{F}_{0m_{0}}(x)
can be calculated with
\mathcal{O}(1)
complexities following the Gaussian linear model, and thus we can calculate the corresponding
\hat{e}_{u}(x)=c_{\rho}\{\hat{F}_{1m_{1}}(x),\hat{F}_{0m_{0}}(x)\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}\hat{f}_{zm_{z}}(x)
with
\mathcal{O}(1)
complexity, and thus

\sup_{(x,u)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{M}^{2}}\mathrm{CT}\{\hat{e}_{u}(x)\}=\mathcal{O}(1).

Example 2

Suppose we estimate
\mu_{z}(x,m)
through the linear regression such that
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)=\hat{\ell}^{\prime\top}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
z\\
m\end{pmatrix}
, where
\hat{\ell}^{\prime}
is a vector of coefficients in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+2}
, and assume
d_{X}
is fixed with
n
. Then we can calculate
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
, for any given
x\in\mathbb{X},z\in\{0,1\},m\in\mathbb{M}
, with
\mathcal{O}(1)
complexity, and thus

\sup_{(x,u)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{M}^{2}}\mathrm{CT}\{\hat{e}_{u}(x)\}=\mathcal{O}(1).

Example 3

Suppose we estimate
\mu_{z}(x,m)
through the multivariate Nadaraya–Watson estimator
[
Nadaraya, 1964
,
Watson, 1964
]
:

\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{K}_{d_{X}+2}\{(x_{i}-x,z_{i}-z,m_{i}-m)/h\}y_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{K}_{d_{X}+2}\{(x_{i}-x,z_{i}-z,m_{i}-m)/h\}},

where
h>0
is a pre-specified bandwidth,
\mathcal{K}_{d_{X}+2}(\cdot)
is any kernel function mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+2}
to
\mathbb{R}
, and for concreteness, here we consider
\mathcal{K}_{d_{X}+2}(\cdot)
to be the
(d_{X}+2)
-dimensional standard Gaussian kernel. Then for any given
x\in\mathbb{X},z\in\{0,1\},m\in\mathbb{M}
, one can see that calculating
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
needs
\mathcal{O}(n)
complexity, and thus

\sup_{(x,z,m)\in\mathbb{X}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{M}}\mathrm{CT}\{\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)\}=\mathcal{O}(n).

When we estimate
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
following Example
1
and Example
2
, respectively, we have
r_{n}=\max\{\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(1)\}=\mathcal{O}(1)
. When we estimate
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
following Example
1
and Example
3
, respectively, we have
r_{n}=\max\{\mathcal{O}(1),\mathcal{O}(n)\}=\mathcal{O}(n)
.

Theorem
S7
demonstrates the concrete computational complexity bound of calculating all numerical integrations for our proposed estimator, with approximation errors of all numerical integrations controlled by
o(n^{-1/2})
.

Theorem S7

Consider the following regularity conditions. For some universal constant
C>0
, assume for any
i\in[n]
, all
\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})
,
\partial\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})/\partial m_{1}
,
\partial\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\partial m_{0}
, and
\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},m_{1})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},m_{0})\}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})
are uniformly bounded by
C
over
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and assume

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial^{2}g(u)}{\partial m_{1}\partial m_{0}}\right|du,\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial g(u)}{\partial m_{1}}\right|du,\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial g(u)}{\partial m_{0}}\right|du,\,\sup_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{\partial g(u)}{\partial m_{0}}\right|dm_{0},\,\sup_{m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{\partial g(u)}{\partial m_{1}}\right|dm_{1}\leq C,

(S15)

for
g(u)\in\{\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i}),\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},m_{1})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},m_{0})\}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\}
. Let
h\succsim n^{-1/2}
,
\mathcal{K}
be the standard Gaussian kernel, and
r_{n}
is defined in (
S14
).

The event of calculating all the numerical integrations in
\{\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\{\llbracket\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},{\bm{\cdot}})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},{\scriptstyle\star})\}

\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\rrbracket_{u^{*}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\hat{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(Z_{i})}(M_{i},X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
such that all approximation errors of the numerical integrations are bounded by
o(n^{-1/2})
, can be addressed by a procedure with computational complexity bounded by

\mathcal{O}\{(\log n)^{2+\epsilon}n^{2}r_{n}\}
, for any fixed
\epsilon>0
.

The proof of Theorem
S7
is deferred to Section
S8.8
. Below, we briefly discuss the conditions in Theorem
S7
. In general, the regularity conditions on
\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})
and
\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},m_{1})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},m_{0})\}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})
in Theorem
S7
can be satisfied if
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
is derived from a smooth two-dimensional density family and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
is uniformly bounded with a bounded first-order derivative with respect to
m
for all
(x,z,m)\in\mathbb{X}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{M}
.
For example, suppose
c_{\rho}(\cdot)
is a Gaussian copula with
\rho
strictly bounded away from
-1
and
1
, and all nuisance functions are estimated using the same parametric strategy described in Section
6.1
for our numerical experiments. Then, the regularity conditions are satisfied. Additionally, Theorem
6
establishes that
h\succsim n^{-1/2}
is necessary to ensure that
\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}
remains a consistent nonparametric estimator of
\tau_{u^{*}}^{*}
. Thus, the requirement
h\succsim n^{-1/2}
in Theorem
S7
is also mild.

Theorem
S7
suggests that if
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
are estimated using relatively simple machine learning algorithms with
r_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, the numerical integrations can be computed in approximately
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time (ignoring logarithmic factors), making the computation quite efficient. However, employing more complex estimation methods for
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
and
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
can increase computation time. For instance, in Examples
1
and
3
, we have
r_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, resulting in a nearly
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
complexity. However, if
\hat{e}_{u}(x)
follows Example
1
while
\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)
follows Example
3
, then
r_{n}=\mathcal{O}(n)
, increasing the total complexity to approximately
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
. From a practical perspective, selecting computationally efficient machine learning algorithms for nuisance function estimation, leading to a smaller
r_{n}
can enhance the overall computational efficiency of our proposed estimator.

S5
Enhanced robustness under linear
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}

In this section, we present Theorem
S8
, which shows the enhanced robustness of our proposed estimator with respect to nuisance function specification and sensitivity parameter selection when
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}
follows a linear model. The implications of Theorem
S8
can be summarized by the following two key takeaway messages.

(i)

Suppose
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}
is linear, and all identification assumptions (Assumptions
1
-
3
) and mild regularity conditions hold. Our proposed estimator has the robustness surpassing the triple robustness. That is, whenever
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}
is correctly specified, or both
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tp}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{ps}}
are correctly specified, our proposed estimator identifies the true Cont.PCE correctly with vanishing
h
.

(ii)

Suppose
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}
is linear, and two causal identification assumptions (Assumptions
1
-
2
) and mild regularity conditions hold. When
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}
is correctly specified in estimation, even if one does not choose the true sensitivity parameters
c_{\rho}(\cdot,\star)
and
\rho
, our proposed estimator can still identify the true Cont.PCE correctly with vanishing
h
. This indicates that when the outcome model is linear or nearly linear, the choice of sensitivity parameters can have a non-sensitive effect on the estimation bias of our proposed estimator towards the true Cont.PCE.

Theorem S8

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
2

hold, and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{om}}
follows a linear model:

\mu_{z}(x,m)=\lambda^{\top}(1,x^{\top},z,m)\text{ for some }\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{d_{X}+3})^{\top}.

Denote the functional limit
\bar{f}_{zm}(x)
such that
\hat{f}_{zm}(x)\rightarrow\bar{f}_{zm}(x)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and
\bar{F}_{zm}(x)=\int_{(-\infty,m]}\bar{f}_{zm^{\prime}}(x)dm^{\prime}
. The selected copula function and correlation parameter for our proposed estimator, are
\bar{c}_{\bar{\rho}}(\cdot,\star)
and
\bar{\rho}
, respectively. Thus the specified principal model is

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{u}(x)=\bar{c}_{\bar{\rho}}\big\{\bar{F}(M_{1}=m_{1}\mid X=x),\bar{F}(M_{0}=m_{0}\mid X=x)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}\bar{f}(M_{z}=m_{z}\mid X=x).

The true principal score model is
e_{u}(x)=f(M_{1}=m_{1},M_{0}=m_{0}\mid X=x)=c_{\rho}\big\{F(M_{1}=m_{1}\mid X=x),F(M_{0}=m_{0}\mid X=x)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f(M_{z}=m_{z}\mid X=x)
following Assumption
3
. Also suppose the regularity conditions in Proposition
1
hold, and
\bar{e}_{u}(x)
satisfies Assumption
S6
similarly with
e_{u}(x)
.

When the true copula function and correlation parameter are replaced by
\bar{c}_{\bar{\rho}}(\cdot,\star)
and
\bar{\rho}
for our proposed estimator
\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}
in (
11
), the probabilistic limit of
\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}
becomes

\displaystyle\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[\check{\xi}_{1,u^{*}}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\bar{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\}+\big\llbracket\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,{\bm{\cdot}})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,{\scriptstyle\star})\big\}\bar{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X)\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\bar{e}}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X)\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}},

where
\check{\xi}_{1,u^{*}}(x,z,m)=\{\bar{\pi}_{z}(x)\bar{f}_{zm}(x)\}^{-1}{(-1)^{z+1}\bar{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(z)}(x,m)}
, and
\bar{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(1)}(m,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(m,m_{0})

\bar{e}_{(m,m_{0})}(x)dm_{0}
,
\bar{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(0)}(m,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(m_{1},m)\bar{e}_{(m_{1},m)}(x)dm_{1}
. Then when either
\bar{\mu}=\mu
or
(\bar{\pi},\bar{e})=(\pi,e)
, we have

\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}={\tau}^{*}_{u^{*}}.

The proof of Theorem
S8
is deferred to Section
S8.9
.

S6
Comparison with
Lu et al. [2023]

In this section, we provide a comprehensive comparison of our work with
Lu et al. [2023]
. To facilitate comparison, we first provide a brief overview of
Lu et al. [2023]
using our paper’s notation. We then detail the major differences of two papers, emphasizing the fundamentally distinct approaches in approximating and estimating Cont.PCE: the local smoothing-based approach versus the global projection-based approach.

Overview of the method in
Lu et al. [2023]

To ensure self-containment, we first provide a brief overview of
Lu et al. [2023]
. Their main paper focuses on the identification and estimation of the average potential outcomes:

m_{z}(u)=\mathbb{E}(Y_{z}\mid U=u),

for
z=0,1
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The Cont.PCE is connected to the average potential outcomes via

{\tau}_{u}^{*}=\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}-Y_{0}\mid U=u)=m_{1}(u)-m_{0}(u).

Similar to Cont.PCE, the average potential outcomes are also non-pathwise differentiable.
Lu et al. [2023]
propose projecting
m_{z}(u)
onto parametric working models
f_{z}(u;\eta)
for
z\in\{0,1\}
, where
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
represents the working model parameter.

Specifically, they approximate
m_{1}(u)
and
m_{0}(u)
using functional forms
f_{1}(u;\eta_{1})
and
f_{0}(u;\eta_{0})
, respectively, where

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=\arg\min_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\mathbb{E}\Big[w_{1}(U)\{m_{1}(U)-f_{1}(U;\eta)\}^{2}\Big],\quad\eta_{0}=\arg\min_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\mathbb{E}\Big[w_{0}(U)\{m_{0}(U)-f_{0}(U;\eta)\}^{2}\Big],

(S16)

with user-specified weight functions
w_{1}(u)
and
w_{0}(u)
, e.g., setting
w_{1}(u)=w_{0}(u)=1
for all
u\in\mathbb{M}^{2}
. This reduces the problem of approximating
m_{z}(u)
to estimating
\eta_{z}
.

Lu et al. [2023,

§

3,

§

4]
develop various identification and estimation methods for
\eta_{z}
, including the EIF-based estimator
\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}
, obtained as the solution to:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{D}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}(Y_{i},M_{i},Z_{i},X_{i};\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}},\hat{\pi},\hat{e},\hat{\mu}_{1})=0,

(S17)

where
{D}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}(\cdot)
is detailed in
Lu et al. [2023, Lemma 3]
. The semiparametric efficiency of
\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}
is established in
Lu et al. [2023,

§

5]
under the doubly robust condition, ensuring that nuisance functions can be well approximated.

For a general linear working model:

\displaystyle f_{z}(u;\eta)=\eta^{\top}g(u),\quad z\in\{0,1\},

(S18)

where
g(u)
is a pre-specified vector-valued function and
\eta
is the unknown parameter.
Lu et al. [2023,

§

4.2]
provide closed-form solutions for
\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}
and
\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
from (
S17
).

As noted in Remark 1 of
Lu et al. [2023]
, one can also project Cont.PCE directly onto a parametric working model
f_{\tau}(u;\eta)
:

\eta_{\tau}=\arg\min_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\mathbb{E}\left[w_{\tau}(U)\{\tau_{U}^{*}-f_{\tau}(U;\eta)\}^{2}\right],

where
w_{\tau}(u)
is a user-specified weight function.
Lu et al. [2023]
derive the EIF of
\eta_{\tau}
in the general case. For linear working models with identical weight functions,
Lu et al. [2023, Corollary S1]
show that the EIF of
\eta_{1}-\eta_{0}
coincides with that of
\eta_{\tau}
. Thus, the closed-form EIF-based estimators
\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}
and
\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
yield a closed-form EIF-based estimator for
\eta_{\tau}
as
\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}-\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
.

Considered scenario for comparisons

To simplify and clarify our comparisons, we focus on the scenario where all working models are identical and adopt the general linear model in (
S18
), with all weight functions also being identical. Specifically, we define the considered working model as

\displaystyle f(u;\eta)=\eta^{\top}g(u),

for a fixed vector-valued function
g(u):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
, where
q
is a fixed positive integer, and we denote the corresponding weight function by
w(u)
. Accordingly, we assume

f(u;\eta)=f_{1}(u;\eta)=f_{0}(u;\eta)=f_{\tau}(u;\eta),\quad w(u)=w_{1}(u)=w_{0}(u)=w_{\tau}(u).

Under this setting, the efficient estimator for the Cont.PCE projection parameters proposed by
Lu et al. [2023]
is given by

\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}}=\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}-\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}},

which we compare against our proposed Cont.PCE estimator.

I. Difference in non-regular estimand approximation and estimation

To address the non-pathwise differentiability of Cont.PCE, the two papers adopt different approaches to approximate the target causal estimands using regular substitutive estimands. Both approaches develop semiparametric efficient estimators for these substitutive estimands, thereby approximating causal quantities such as Cont.PCE and average potential outcomes. We summarize the details of these two approaches below.

•

Our approach considers Loc.PCE
\tau_{u}
as the regular substitutive estimand, which provides a local functional approximation of Cont.PCE
\tau^{*}_{u}
via a kernel-based method. By focusing on any specific principal strata
u
, we develop a semiparametric efficient estimator for Loc.PCE. If

\tau_{u}\rightarrow\tau_{u}^{*}\quad\text{as}\quad h\to 0,

then our estimator serves as a nonparametric estimator for Cont.PCE.

•

Lu et al. [2023]
instead consider the working model projection
f(u;\eta_{\tau})
, which provides a global approximation of Cont.PCE. They develop the EIF-based estimator
\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}}
for the substitutive estimand
\eta_{\tau}
, and
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
serves as an efficient estimator for the global projection
f(u;\eta_{\tau})
. If the working model
f
correctly specifies Cont.PCE, then
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
also serves as an efficient estimator for Cont.PCE.

The key distinction between our method and
Lu et al. [2023]
lies in the fundamental difference between local smoothing-based and global projection-based estimation for non-regular estimands. Both approaches, along with fully model-based estimation when the non-regular estimand is specified by a parametric model, involve bias-variance trade-offs. We first compare the estimation properties of our method and
Lu et al. [2023]
, then introduce the fully model-based approach and discuss the bias-variance trade-offs among the three methods in the Cont.PCE estimation context.

•

As a local smoothing-based estimator, our proposed estimator
\hat{\tau}_{u}
achieves semiparametric efficiency for Loc.PCE with an
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
convergence rate and further estimates Cont.PCE with an
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/3})
rate, under the regularity conditions of Theorem
6
and the doubly robust condition
r_{\pi}r_{\mu}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
.

•

As a global projection-based estimator,
\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}}
achieves
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
convergence for
\eta_{\tau}
under the regularity conditions in
Lu et al. [2023, Theorem 5]
and the doubly robust assumption. Consequently,
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
efficiently estimates the global projection of Cont.PCE with an
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
rate under (
S18
). However, if the working model does not correctly specify Cont.PCE, a non-vanishing bias will persist between
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
and
\tau_{u}^{*}
as
n\to\infty
, leading to inconsistency in estimating Cont.PCE. The usefulness of
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
then depends on the alignment between the working model and the true Cont.PCE.

•

If the working model in (
S18
) correctly specifies Cont.PCE, then
f(u;{\eta}_{\tau})=\tau^{*}_{u}
, and
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
achieves an
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
convergence rate for Cont.PCE. However, in this case, the projection-based estimator does not generally attain the semiparametric efficiency bound. Specifically, the asymptotic variance bound of
\hat{\eta}_{\tau}
in
Lu et al. [2023, Theorem 5]
depends on the weight function
w(u)
, whereas the semiparametric efficiency bound under the parametric model assumption of Cont.PCE does not. To achieve the semiparametric efficiency bound under the parametric assumption that
\tau_{u}^{*}
can be correctly specified by the parametric working model
f(u;\eta)
, one can further develop a fully model-based estimator for
\tau_{u}^{*}
through the semiparametric statistics framework.

The three estimation approaches: local smoothing-based, global projection-based and fully model-based, exhibit different bias-variance tradeoffs in estimation:

•

The local smoothing-based estimator does not rely on a parametric model for Cont.PCE, avoiding model misspecification bias. However, it may be less efficient with a slower convergence rate than the other two methods, when the Cont.PCE is indeed correctly specified by the parametric working model.

•

The global projection-based estimator balances efficiency and robustness. It can be more efficient than the local smoothing-based estimator when the parametric model assumption holds, while still providing an interpretable approximation of the Cont.PCE projection when the assumption does not hold.

•

The fully model-based estimator is the most efficient when the parametric model assumption holds but loses interpretability if the model is misspecified, as it may not converge to some meaningful projection of Cont.PCE; see also
Kennedy et al. [2019,

§

3.1]
.

Remark S8

If we let
g(u)
in (
S18
) be a set of basis functions (e.g., power series, B-spline basis, or wavelet basis; see
Eubank [1999]
) and allow its dimension
q
to grow as
n\to\infty
, then
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
may serve as a nonparametric spline estimator for Cont.PCE. This enables nonparametric estimation of the Cont.PCE surface without requiring that (
S18
) correctly specifies
\tau_{u}^{*}
for any finite
q
. However, the convergence rate of
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}})
will generally be slower than
\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
due to the increasing dimension
q
. The difference between our proposed estimator and such a spline estimator parallels the classic difference between kernel and spline estimators in nonparametric regression. Specifically, our proposed estimator is point-wise, and its accuracy relies on the point-wise smoothness of the Cont.PCE surface. In contrast, the accuracy of the spline estimator relies on the global approximation error of the spline basis towards the Cont.PCE surface.

II. Difference in computations

Recall that
\hat{\eta}_{\tau,\mathrm{eif}}=\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}-\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
. As discussed in the overview, the EIF-based estimator
\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}
for
z\in\{1,0\}
is obtained from (
S17
), with its computational complexity depending on the working model specification. When
f_{z}
is a constant or follows the general linear model (
S18
),
Lu et al. [2023]
derive closed-form solutions for
\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}
. For instance, under the general linear model (
S18
) for
f_{1}
, they show that

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}_{1,\text{eif}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{A}_{i2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{A}_{i1}},

(S19)

with

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{i1}=

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{i}}{\hat{\pi}_{1}(X_{i})}\left[\frac{\int\hat{G}_{1}(M_{i},m_{0},X_{i};\hat{\mu}_{1})\,\mathrm{d}m_{0}}{\hat{f}_{1M_{i}}(X_{i})}-\iint\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},m_{0},X_{i};\hat{\mu}_{1})\hat{r}_{u}(m_{1},m_{0},M_{i},X_{i})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}\mathrm{d}m_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1-Z_{i}}{\hat{\pi}_{0}(X_{i})}\left[\frac{\int\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},M_{i},X_{i};\hat{\mu}_{1})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}}{\hat{f}_{0M_{i}}(X_{i})}-\iint\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},m_{0},X_{i};\hat{\mu}_{1})\hat{r}_{v}(m_{1},m_{0},M_{i},X_{i})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}\mathrm{d}m_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\iint\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},m_{0},X_{i};\hat{\mu}_{1})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}\mathrm{d}m_{0}+\frac{Z_{i}}{\hat{\pi}_{1}(X_{i})}\frac{\int\hat{G}_{1}(M_{i},m_{0},X_{i};1)\,\mathrm{d}m_{0}}{\hat{f}_{1M_{i}}(X_{i})}\{Y_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{1}(M_{i},X_{i})\},

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{i2}=

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{i}}{\hat{\pi}_{1}(X_{i})}\left[\frac{\int\hat{G}_{1}(M_{i},m_{0},X_{i};g_{1})\,\mathrm{d}m_{0}}{\hat{f}_{1M_{i}}(X_{i})}-\iint\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},m_{0},X_{i};g_{1})\hat{r}_{u}(m_{1},m_{0},M_{i},X_{i})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}\mathrm{d}m_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1-Z_{i}}{\hat{\pi}_{0}(X_{i})}\left[\int\frac{\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},M_{i},X_{i};g_{1})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}}{\hat{f}_{0M_{i}}(X_{i})}-\iint\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},m_{0},X_{i};g_{1})\hat{r}_{v}(m_{1},m_{0},M_{i},X_{i})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}\mathrm{d}m_{0}\right]

\displaystyle+\iint\hat{G}_{1}(m_{1},m_{0},X_{i};g_{1})\,\mathrm{d}m_{1}\mathrm{d}m_{0};

the definitions of some additional functions like
\hat{G}_{1}(\cdot)
and
\hat{r}_{u}(\cdot)
can be found in
Lu et al. [2023,

§

4.2]
, and similar results also hold for
\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
. Comparing (
11
) and (
S19
), we observe that both approaches require computing
\mathcal{O}(n)
two-dimensional numerical integrations. Thus, the computational complexity analysis of numerical integrations, similar to that presented in Section
S4
in the revision, applies to
\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}
as well. Overall, the computational complexities of obtaining
\hat{\eta}_{z,\mathrm{eif}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{u}
are comparable.

When evaluating Cont.PCE
\tau^{*}_{u}
across a large number of different principal strata, the method proposed by
Lu et al. [2023]
can be computationally more efficient. This is because
\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}
and
\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
need to be computed only once, and the linear model
f(u;\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}-\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}})
is generally straightforward to evaluate for each
u
. In contrast, our proposed estimator
\hat{\tau}_{u}
must be computed separately for each
u
, increasing the computational cost.

For non-linear working models for
f_{1}
and
f_{0}
, (
S17
) may not have a closed-form solution, requiring numerical methods such as Newton’s method to approximate
\hat{\eta}_{1,\mathrm{eif}}
and
\hat{\eta}_{0,\mathrm{eif}}
. In contrast, our proposed estimator always has a closed-form solution, as shown in (
11
), and does not require pre-specification of working models, making it very easy to implement in practical applications.

III. Difference in the application foci

Our paper has a distinct application motivation and focus–surrogate evaluation–differentiating it from
Lu et al. [2023]
, whose real data analysis primarily focuses on causal median analysis. For instance, Section
2.2
of our paper provides a detailed discussion on the connection between Cont.PCE and key concepts in surrogate evaluation, including associative and dissociative effects, causal sufficiency, and causal necessity. We further illustrate how Cont.PCE surfaces can be leveraged for surrogate evaluation through a simple and intuitive numerical example in Section
2.2
.
Beyond the conceptual framework, our real data analysis also centers on the surrogate evaluation of short-term CD4 count in ACTG 175, providing a comprehensive empirical investigation.

S7
Proof of Proposition
1

S7.1
Regularity conditions

We list the three regularity conditions for Proposition
1
as follows.

Assumption S5

The second-order partial derivative of
\mu_{z}(x,m)
with respect to
m
is uniformly bounded for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
,
m_{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
z=0,1
.

Assumption S6

All second-order partial derivatives of
f\{(M_{1},M_{0})=u\mid X=x\}=e_{u}(x)
with respect to
u
, are uniformly bounded for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
z=0,1
.

Assumption S7

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1},\delta_{0})d\delta_{1}d\delta_{0}=1
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta_{z}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1},\delta_{0})d\delta_{1}d\delta_{0}=0
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\delta_{1}|^{c_{1}}|\delta_{0}|^{c_{0}}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1},\delta_{0})

d\delta_{1}d\delta_{0}<+\infty
for all integers
c_{1},c_{0}
such that
c_{1}+c_{0}\leq 4
and
z=0,1
.

The bounded second-order partial derivatives of
\mu_{z}(x,m)
and
e_{u}(x)
in Assumptions
S5
and
S6
are standard in the nonparametric kernel regression literature. Assumption
S7
for
\mathcal{K}(\cdot,\cdot)
is also mild and can be typically satisfied by the two-dimensional Gaussian Kernel, box kernel, Epanechnikov kernel, among others. See e.g.
Fan [1993]
and
Wasserman [2006]
.

S7.2
Main proof of Proposition
1

Some technical arguments are standard in the nonparametric kernel regression literature for bias control, and thus we will omit the corresponding details; See, e.g.
Fan [1993]
for more discussions. For the ease of exposition, denote
\Delta_{u}(x)=\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})
for any
u=(m_{1},m_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
. By definition, one has

\displaystyle\tau_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+2}}\Delta_{u}(x)\frac{k_{u^{*}}(u)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}e_{u}(x)f(x)dm_{1}dm_{0}dx

(S20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+2}}\Delta_{u^{*}+(\delta_{1},\delta_{0})}(x)\frac{k_{u^{*}}\{u^{*}+(\delta_{1},\delta_{0})\}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}e_{u^{*}+(\delta_{1},\delta_{0})}(x)f(x)d\delta_{1}d\delta_{0}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+2}}\Delta_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)\frac{k_{u^{*}}\{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}\}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}e_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)f(x)h^{2}d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+2}}\Delta_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)\frac{\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}e_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)f(x)d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})e_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}dx,

where we denote
\delta^{\prime}=(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})
. By Taylor expansion, one has for any given
x\in\mathbb{X}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(x,m_{1}^{*})+h\delta_{1}^{\prime}\frac{\partial\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})}{\partial m_{1}}\Big|_{m_{1}=m_{1}^{*}}+\frac{(h\delta_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})}{\partial^{2}m_{1}}\Big|_{m_{1}=m_{1}^{*}+h_{1}\delta_{1}^{\prime}}

(S21)

\displaystyle-\mu_{0}(x,m_{0}^{*})-h\delta_{0}^{\prime}\frac{\partial\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})}{\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{m_{0}=m_{0}^{*}}-\frac{(h\delta_{0}^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})}{\partial^{2}m_{0}}\Big|_{m_{0}=m_{0}^{*}+h_{0}\delta_{0}^{\prime}}

where
|h_{z}|\leq h
and
\sgn(h_{z})=\sgn(h)
for
z=0,1
;
\text{sgn}(\cdot)
is the sign function. Similarly, we have,

\displaystyle e_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{u^{*}}(x)+h\delta_{1}^{\prime}\frac{\partial e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}}+h\delta_{0}^{\prime}\frac{\partial e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}}

(S22)

\displaystyle+\frac{(h\delta_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}e_{u}(x)}{\partial^{2}m_{1}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}+h_{10}\delta^{\prime}}+\frac{(h\delta_{0}^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}e_{u}(x)}{\partial^{2}m_{0}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}+h_{10}\delta^{\prime}}+\frac{h^{2}\delta_{1}^{\prime}\delta_{0}^{\prime}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}+h_{10}\delta^{\prime}},

for some
|h_{10}|\leq h
and
\sgn(h_{10})=\sgn(h)
. Substituting the right-hand side of (
S20
) with (
S21
) and (
S22
), we have

\displaystyle\tau_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)e_{u^{*}}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}dx

(S23)

\displaystyle+h\cdot\Bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)\frac{\partial e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}}_{=0}dx+\cdots

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}e_{u^{*}}(x)\delta_{0}^{\prime}\frac{\partial\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})}{\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{m_{0}=m_{0}^{*}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta_{0}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}}_{=0}dx\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\Bigg\{h^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\Big\{\frac{\partial e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}}\frac{\partial\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})}{\partial m_{1}}\Big\}\Big|_{u=u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta_{1}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}dx+\cdots

\displaystyle-\frac{h^{4}}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})}{\partial^{2}m_{0}}\Big|_{m_{0}=m_{0}^{*}+h_{0}\delta_{0}^{\prime}}\frac{\partial^{2}e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}+h_{10}\delta^{\prime}}\delta_{1}^{\prime}(\delta_{0}^{\prime})^{3}\mathcal{K}(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime})d\delta^{\prime}_{1}d\delta^{\prime}_{0}dx\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)e_{u^{*}}(x)dx+hR_{1}+h^{2}R_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)e_{u^{*}}(x)dx+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

where the last equality is because that, by the regularity conditions and the fact that
[\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}]^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(1)
(see (
S27
) in the following), we can derive
R_{1}=0
and
R_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
when
h\rightarrow 0
with standard arguments for controlling the bias of the kernel nonparametric regression
[
Fan, 1993
]
.

Next, we show
|1/\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}-1/e_{u^{*}}|=\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
. By the Taylor expansion, one has

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}}-\frac{1}{e_{u^{*}}}\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\big|\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}-e_{u^{*}}\big|

(S24)

for some
\xi
between
\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}
and
e_{u^{*}}
. Similar to (
S20
)–(
S22
), one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\mid X\}\big]

(S25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}f(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{h^{2}}\mathcal{K}\Big(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\Big)e_{u}(x)dm_{1}dm_{0}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}f(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{K}\big(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime}\big)e_{u^{*}+h\delta^{\prime}}(x)d\delta_{1}^{\prime}d\delta_{0}^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}f(x)e_{u^{*}}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{K}\big(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime}\big)d\delta_{1}^{\prime}d\delta_{0}^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle+h\cdot\Bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}f(x)\frac{\partial e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}\big(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime}\big)d\delta_{1}^{\prime}d\delta_{0}^{\prime}dx}_{=0}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}f(x)\frac{\partial e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\delta_{0}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}\big(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime}\big)d\delta_{1}^{\prime}d\delta_{0}^{\prime}dx}_{=0}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+h^{2}\cdot\Bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}e_{u}(x)}{\partial^{2}m_{1}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}+h_{10}\delta^{\prime}}(\delta_{1}^{\prime})^{2}\mathcal{K}\big(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime}\big)d\delta_{1}^{\prime}d\delta_{0}^{\prime}dx+\cdots

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\frac{f(x)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}e_{u}(x)}{\partial m_{1}\partial m_{0}}\Big|_{u=u^{*}+h_{10}\delta^{\prime}}\delta_{1}^{\prime}\delta_{0}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}\big(\delta_{1}^{\prime},\delta_{0}^{\prime}\big)d\delta_{1}^{\prime}d\delta_{0}^{\prime}dx\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}f(x)e_{u^{*}}(x)dx+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{u^{*}}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

(S26)

Thus
|\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}-e_{u^{*}}|=\mathcal{O}(h^{2})=o(1)
when
h\rightarrow 0
, which implies
\xi\rightarrow e_{u^{*}}
. Thus when
h\rightarrow 0
, we have,

\displaystyle\xi^{-1}\precsim 1

and

\displaystyle[\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}]^{-1}\precsim 1,

(S27)

as
e_{u^{*}}>0
is fixed. Then (
S24
)–(
S27
) imply,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}}-\frac{1}{e_{u^{*}}}\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

(S28)

Recalling (
S23
), one has

\displaystyle\tau_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)\{e_{u^{*}}(x)/e_{u^{*}}\}f(x)dx+\Big[\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}-\frac{1}{e_{u^{*}}}\Big]\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)e_{u^{*}}(x)f(x)dx+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})

(S29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)f(X=x\mid U=u^{*})dx+\underbrace{\Big[\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}-\frac{1}{e_{u^{*}}}\Big]e_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)f(X=x\mid U=u^{*})dx}_{=R_{3}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

where we note
\{e_{u^{*}}(x)/e_{u^{*}}\}f(x)=f\{(M_{1},M_{0})=u^{*}\mid X=x\}f(X=x)/f\{(M_{1},M_{0})=u^{*}\}=f\{X=x\mid U=u^{*}\}
. Furthermore, under Assumptions
1
–
2
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)f\{X=x\mid(M_{1},M_{0})=u^{*}\}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m^{*}_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m^{*}_{0})\mid U=u^{*}\big\}

(S30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{u^{*}}^{*},

by an argument same as (
S32
) in the Proof of Theorem
1
. Combining (
S28
)–(
S30
), one has

\displaystyle|R_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}-\frac{1}{e_{u^{*}}}\Big|\cdot e_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}}\Delta_{u^{*}}(x)f\{X=x\mid U=u^{*}\}dx

(S31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\big\}}-\frac{1}{e_{u^{*}}}\Big|\cdot e_{u^{*}}\tau_{u^{*}}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

Combining (
S29
)–(
S31
), we have
\tau_{u^{*}}=\tau_{u^{*}}^{*}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
and thus

\big|\tau_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}}^{*}\big|=\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
as desired.

\Box

S8
Proofs of main theorems

Throughout the proofs, we will use
f(\cdot)
to denote the probability
density function for continuous random variables. We will use the law of
total expectation repeatedly and will mark the steps as
LOTE
. We first recall some notations in the main text,

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}\pi_{z}(X)&=&\mathbb{P}(Z=z\mid X),&e_{u}(X)&=&f(U=u\mid X),\\
e_{u}&=&\mathbb{E}\{e_{u}(X)\}=f(U=u),&\mu_{z}(X,m)&=&\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X,Z=z,M=m),\\
f_{zm}(X)&=&f(M=m\mid X,Z=z),&F_{zm}(X)&=&\mathbb{P}(M\leq m\mid X,Z=z),\end{array}

for
z=0,1
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
u=(m_{1},m_{0})
. Under Assumptions
1
and
3
, we have

\displaystyle e_{u}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\rho}\big\{F(M_{1}=m_{1}\mid X),F(M_{0}=m_{0}\mid X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f(M_{z}=m_{z}\mid X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\rho}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X).

\square

S8.1
Proof of Theorem
1

We first introduce the following lemma from
Jiang et al. [2022, Lemma S3]
.

Lemma S1

Let
A
and
B
be two random variables with densities
f_{A}(\cdot)
and
f_{B}(\cdot)
. For any function
h(\cdot)
with
\mathbb{E}\{h(A)\}<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{h(A)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\frac{f_{A}(B)}{f_{B}(B)}h(B)\Bigg\}.

By the law of the expectation (LOTE), we have

\displaystyle\tau^{*}_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(Y_{1}-Y_{0}\mid U=u^{*}\Big)

(S32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(Y_{1}-Y_{0}\mid X,U=u^{*}\big)\mid U=u^{*}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\{Y_{1}\mid X,U=u^{*}\}-\mathbb{E}\{Y_{0}\mid X,U=u^{*}\}\mid U=u^{*}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\{Y_{1}\mid X,M_{1}=m_{1}^{*}\}-\mathbb{E}\{Y_{0}\mid X,M_{0}=m_{0}^{*}\}\mid U=u^{*}\Big]\quad\text{(Assumption \ref{assump:weak-pi})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\{Y_{1}\mid X,Z=1,M_{1}=m_{1}^{*}\}-\mathbb{E}\{Y_{0}\mid X,Z=0,M_{0}=m_{0}^{*}\}\mid U=u^{*}\Big]\quad\text{(Assumption \ref{assump:TAignorability})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X,Z=1,M=m_{1}^{*})-\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X,Z=0,M=m_{0}^{*})\mid U=u^{*}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m^{*}_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m^{*}_{0})\mid U=u^{*}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{f(X\mid U=u^{*})}{f(X)}\{\mu_{1}(X,m^{*}_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m^{*}_{0})\}\Big]

(S33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{f(U=u^{*}\mid X)f(X)}{f(U=u^{*})f(X)}\{\mu_{1}(X,m^{*}_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m^{*}_{0})\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{e_{u^{*}}(X)}{e_{u^{*}}}\{\mu_{1}(X,m^{*}_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m^{*}_{0})\}\Big],

where in (
S33
) we use Lemma
S1
with
A=X\mid U=u^{*}
and
B=X
. By Assumptions
1
and
3
,
e_{u^{*}}(x)
can be further identified as

\displaystyle e_{u^{*}}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(M_{1}=m^{*}_{1},M_{0}=m^{*}_{0}\mid X)

(S34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\rho}\big\{F(M_{1}=m^{*}_{1}\mid X),F(M_{0}=m^{*}_{0}\mid X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f(M_{z}=m^{*}_{z}\mid X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\rho}\big\{F(M_{1}=m^{*}_{1}\mid X,Z=1),F(M_{0}=m^{*}_{0}\mid X,Z=0)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f(M_{z}=m^{*}_{z}\mid X,Z=z)

(Assumption
1
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\rho}\big\{F_{1m^{*}_{1}}(X),F_{0m^{*}_{0}}(X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm^{*}_{z}}(X).

\square

S8.2
Proof of Theorem
2

By the law of the expectation (LOTE), we have

\displaystyle\tau_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\{\mu_{1}(X,M_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,M_{0})\big\}\frac{k_{u^{*}}(U)}{\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}}\Big]

(S35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\{\mu_{1}(X,M_{1})-\mu_{1}(X,M_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(U)\mid X\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Bigg],

We also have

\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\mid X\}]=\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}.

Combining above results and recalling the definition of
\llbracket\,\cdot\,\rrbracket
in (
7
), the identification formula follows, and
e_{u}(X)
can be identified same as (
S34
).

\square

S8.3
Proof of Theorem
3

S8.3.1
Preliminary and auxiliary lemmas

To obtain the EIFs, we consider some arbitrary one-dimensional parametric submodel of the whole data density, denoted by
\{f_{\theta}(V=v)\}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}
. Let
f_{\theta}(v)
contain the truth
f(v)
at
\theta=0
, i.e.,
f_{0}(v)=f(v)
. We use
s_{\theta}(\cdot)
and
s(\cdot)
to denote the corresponding score functions, e.g.,
s_{\theta}(x)=\partial\log f_{\theta}(X=x)/\partial\theta
and
s(x)=\partial\log f_{\theta}(X=x)/\partial\theta\mid_{\theta=0}
. The submodel likelihood is factorized as

\displaystyle f_{\theta}(V=v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\theta}(X=x)f_{\theta}(Z=z\mid X=x)f_{\theta}(M=m\mid X=x,Z=z)f_{\theta}(Y=y\mid X=x,Z=z,M=m).

Under the scenario of nonparametric estimation, the score function under submodel can be decomposed as

\displaystyle s_{\theta}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{\theta}(x)+s_{\theta}(z\mid x)+s_{\theta}(m\mid x,z)+s_{\theta}(y\mid x,z,m).

Then the tangent space is

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{1}\oplus H_{2}\oplus H_{3}\oplus H_{4}

(S37)

where
\oplus
is the set sum and,

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle=\{h(x)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X)\}=0\},

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle=\{h(x,z)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X,Z)\mid X\}=0\},

\displaystyle H_{3}

\displaystyle=\{h(x,z,m)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X,Z,M)\mid X,Z\}=0\},

\displaystyle H_{4}

\displaystyle=\{h(x,z,m,y)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X,Z,M,Y)\mid X,Z,M\}=0\}.

Based on the semiparametric theory (see e.g.
Bickel et al. [1993]
,
Jiang et al. [2022]
), we have that under the nonparametric scenario (Scenario (iii)) with a tangent space like (
S37
), EIF of target parameter
\tau_{u^{*}}
is the unique function
\phi(\cdot)
(up to the almost surely equivalence) satisfying,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\tau_{u^{*},\theta}\,\Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\phi(V)\cdot s(V)\Big\}.

(S38)

Under the scenario of semiparametric estimation with known or parametric
\mathcal{M}_{{\rm ps}}
(Scenarios (i) and (ii)), due to the assumption that the distribution (or the parametric distribution model) of
M\mid X,T
is already known, the corresponding tangent space is more restrictive in comparison with (
S37
). As a result, the
\phi(\cdot)
satisfying (
S38
) is no longer unique. Any
\phi(\cdot)
satisfying (
S38
) serves as an influence function (IF) of
\tau_{u^{*}}
, but is not necessary to be an EIF. To show that the
\phi(\cdot)
we derive under Scenarios (i) or (ii) are indeed EIFs, we further verify that the derived
\phi(\cdot)
belong to the corresponding semiparametric tangent spaces; Technical details are deferred to Section
S8.3.4
. Different from the order of Theorem
3
, during the proof, we first derive
\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)
under Scenario (iii) in Section
S8.3.2
, based on which we further derive the EIFs under Scenarios (ii) and (i) in Sections
S8.3.3
and
S8.3.4
, respectively.

We list two technical lemmas that will be used in the main proof.

Lemma S2

Suppose function
\xi=\xi(A_{1},A_{2})
, where
A_{1},A_{2}
are random variables belonging to
V
, and
A_{1},A_{2}
have no overlapping. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{1}|A_{2})\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big[\xi(A_{1},A_{2})-\mathbb{E}\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\}\big]\cdot s(V)\big].

Proof of Lemma
S2
.
By property of score function, we have
s(A_{1}\mid A_{2})=s(A_{1},A_{2})-s(A_{2})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{1}\mid A_{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{1},A_{2})\Big\}-\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{2})\Big\}.

(S39)

If
(A_{1},A_{2})
do not contain all the covariates in
V
, we let
A_{3}
be the rest of the covariates in
V
such that
A_{3}=V\setminus(A_{1},A_{2})
. For the first term of (
S39
), we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{3}\mid A_{1},A_{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{3}\mid A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{1},A_{2}\big\}\Big]

(S40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{s(A_{3}\mid A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{1},A_{2}\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot 0\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where the third equality is by the property of score function as
\mathbb{E}\{s(A_{3}\mid A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{1},A_{2}\}=0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{1},A_{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{1},A_{2})\Big\}+\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{3}\mid A_{1},A_{2})\Big\}

(S41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot\big\{s(A_{1},A_{2})+s(A_{3}\mid A_{1},A_{2})\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(V)\Big\},

where the first equality is by (
S40
). If
(A_{1},A_{2})
contain all covariates in
V
, then
\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{1},A_{2})\big\}=\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi\cdot s(V)\big\}
trivially.

For the second term of (
S39
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\cdot s(A_{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[s(A_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}\Big]\quad\text{(LOTE)}

(S42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[s(A_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[s(A_{1},A_{3}\mid A_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\{s(A_{2})+s(A_{1},A_{3}\mid A_{2})\big\}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big(\xi\mid A_{2}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\xi\mid A_{2}\big)\cdot s(V)\Big\},

where the second equality holds because

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[s(A_{1},A_{3}\mid A_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[s(A_{1},A_{3}\mid A_{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}\mid A_{2}\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi(A_{1},A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big\{s(A_{1},A_{3}\mid A_{2})\mid A_{2}\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

by the property of score function as
\mathbb{E}\big\{s(A_{1},A_{3}\mid A_{2})\mid A_{2}\big\}=0
. Note here if
(A_{1},A_{2})
contain all the covariates in
V
,
A_{3}
can be empty. Combing (
S39
), (
S41
), (
S42
), the desired result follows immediately.

\Box

Lemma S3

Suppose
N_{\theta}
and
D_{\theta}
are differentiable with respect to
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\partial N_{\theta}/\partial\theta\mid_{\theta=0}=\mathbb{E}\{\phi_{N}(V)s(V)\}
and
\partial D_{\theta}/\partial\theta\mid_{\theta=0}=\mathbb{E}\{\phi_{D}(V)s(V)\}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\frac{N_{\theta}}{D_{\theta}}\ \Big|_{\theta=0}=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big\{\frac{1}{D_{0}}\phi_{N}(V)-\frac{N_{0}}{D^{2}_{0}}\phi_{D}(V)\Big\}\cdot s(V)\Bigg].

(S43)

Proof of Lemma
S3
.

See Lemma S2 in
Jiang et al. [2022]
.
\Box

S8.3.2
Derivation of
\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)
under Scenario (iii)

For the nonparametric scenario, by verifying condition (
S38
), we derive the EIFs of the denominator and numerator of
\tau_{u^{*}}
’s identification formula in (
8
), respectively. We then combine these EIFs together and derive the desired EIF of
\tau_{u^{*}}
based on Lemma
S3
.

Step 1 (EIF of
\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}
):
Based on the submodel introduced before, we can write
\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big\}\ \Big|_{\theta=0}.

(S44)

In (
S44
), obtain the derivative with respect to
\theta
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}du\Big\}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(X)\Big\}.

Now we take a careful look at
\partial e^{(\theta)}_{u}(x)/\partial\theta\mid_{\theta=0}
. By definition (
3
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}e^{(\theta)}_{u}(x)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Big[c_{\rho}\Big\{F^{(\theta)}_{1m_{1}}(x),F^{(\theta)}_{0m_{0}}(x)\Big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}^{(\theta)}(x)\Big]\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}c_{\rho}^{(z^{\prime})}\Big\{F_{1m_{1}}(x),F_{0m_{0}}(x)\Big\}\Big\{\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(x)\Big\}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}F^{(\theta)}_{z^{\prime}m_{z^{\prime}}}(x)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle+\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(x),F_{0m_{0}}(x)\}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}f_{z^{\prime}m_{z^{\prime}}}^{(\theta)}(x)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}\cdot f_{1-z^{\prime}m_{1-z^{\prime}}}(x).

For
z^{\prime}=0,1
, since
F_{z^{\prime}m_{z^{\prime}}}^{(\theta)}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{m_{z^{\prime}}}f^{(\theta)}(M_{z^{\prime}}=m\mid X=x)dm
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}F_{z^{\prime}m_{z^{\prime}}}^{(\theta)}(x)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{I}(m\leq m_{z^{\prime}})\frac{\partial f^{(\theta)}(M_{z^{\prime}}=m\mid X=x)}{\partial\theta}\ \Big|_{\theta=0}dm

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{I}(m\leq m_{z^{\prime}})\frac{1}{f(M_{z^{\prime}}=m\mid X=x)}\frac{\partial f^{(\theta)}(M_{z^{\prime}}=m\mid X=x)}{\partial\theta}\ \Big|_{\theta=0}f(M_{z^{\prime}}=m\mid X=x)dm

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{I}(m\leq m_{z^{\prime}})\frac{1}{f(M=m\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})}\frac{\partial f^{(\theta)}(M=m\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})}{\partial\theta}\ \Big|_{\theta=0}f(M=m\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime})dm

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\mathbb{I}(M\leq m_{z^{\prime}})s(M\mid X,Z)\mid X,Z=z^{\prime}\Big\},

where the third equality is by
f(M_{z^{\prime}}=m\mid X=x)=f(M=m\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime})
under Assumption
1
. And similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}f^{(\theta)}(M_{z^{\prime}}=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X=x)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{f(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime})}\Bigg\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}f^{(\theta)}(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime})\ \Big|_{\theta=0}\Bigg\}f(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime})f(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X=x,Z=z^{\prime}).

Combining the results above, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Big|_{\theta=0}

(S45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(z^{\prime})}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\Big\{\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)\Big\}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}F^{(\theta)}_{z^{\prime}m_{z^{\prime}}}(X)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}du\Big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}f_{z^{\prime}m_{z^{\prime}}}^{(\theta)}(X)\ \Big|_{\theta=0}\cdot f_{1-z^{\prime}m_{1-z^{\prime}}}(X)du\Big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(X)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(z^{\prime})}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\big\{\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)\big\}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\mathbb{I}(M\leq m_{z^{\prime}})s(M\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})\mid X,Z=z^{\prime}\big\}du\Big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\}\big\{\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)\big\}s(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})du\Big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(X)\Big\}.

By LOTE and Fubini’s theorem, the first term on the right-hand side of (
S45
) equals to

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big[s(M\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1(M\leq m_{z^{\prime}})k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(z^{\prime})}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}

(S46)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)du\mid X,Z=z^{\prime}\Big]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1(Z=z^{\prime})s(M\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})}{\pi_{z^{\prime}}(X)}

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1(M\leq m_{z^{\prime}})k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(z^{\prime})}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)du\mid X\Big]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\frac{1(Z=z^{\prime})s(M\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})}{\pi_{z^{\prime}}(X)}\mathbf{\int}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1(M\leq m_{z^{\prime}})k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(z^{\prime})}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)du\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[s(M\mid X,Z)\cdot\frac{1}{\pi_{Z}(X)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1(M\leq m_{Z})k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(Z)}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)du\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)\cdot s(V)\Big\}\quad(\text{By Lemma \ref{lm:MonXZ} with $A_{1}=M,$ $A_{2}=(X,Z)$}),

recalling

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi_{Z}(X)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}1(M\leq m_{Z})k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(Z)}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)du,

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi_{2}(X,Z,M)-\mathbb{E}\big\{\xi_{2}(X,Z,M)\mid X,Z\big\}.

Note here and in the following, for simplicity, we will omit the footnote
u^{*}
in
\xi_{2,u^{*}}(x,z,m)
,
\xi_{2,u^{*}}^{\prime}(x,z,m)
,
\tilde{\xi}_{2,u^{*}}^{\prime}(z,x,m)
, etc. By definition, for both
z^{\prime}=0
or
1
we have

\displaystyle{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(z^{\prime})}(M=m_{z^{\prime}},X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\}f_{1-z^{\prime}m_{1-z^{\prime}}}(X)dm_{1-z^{\prime}}.

The second term on the right-hand side of (
S45
) equals to

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\}\prod_{z=0,1}f_{zm_{z}}(X)\cdot s(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})du\Big]

(S47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\}f_{1-z^{\prime}m_{1-z^{\prime}}}(X)dm_{1-z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\cdot s(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})f(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})dm_{z^{\prime}}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(z^{\prime})}(X,M=m_{z^{\prime}})s(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})f(M=m_{z^{\prime}}\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})dm_{z^{\prime}}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big\{{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(z^{\prime})}(X,M)s(M\mid X,Z=z^{\prime})\mid X,Z=z^{\prime}\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{1(Z=z^{\prime})}{\pi_{z^{\prime}}(X)}{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(z^{\prime})}(X,M)s(M\mid X,Z)\mid X\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{\sum_{z^{\prime}=1,0}\frac{1(Z=z^{\prime})}{\pi_{z^{\prime}}(X)}{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(z^{\prime})}(X,M)\Big\}s(M\mid X,Z)\Big]\quad\text{(LOTE)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{1}{\pi_{Z}(X)}{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(Z)}(X,M)s(M\mid X,Z)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\xi^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)s(M\mid X,Z)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)s(V)\}\quad(\text{Lemma \ref{lm:MonXZ} with $A_{1}=M$, $A_{2}=(X,Z)$}),

recalling
\xi^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)=\{\pi_{Z}(X)\}^{-1}{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}^{(Z)}(X,M)
and
\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)=\xi^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)-\mathbb{E}\big[\xi^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)\mid X,Z\big]
.

For the third term on the right-hand side of (
S45
), since
s(X)+s(Z,M,Y\mid X)=s(V)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(X)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot\{s(V)-s(Z,M,Y|X)\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(V)\Big\}-\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Big\{s(Z,M,Y|X)\mid X\Big\}}_{=0}\Big]\quad(\text{LOTE})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(V)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)-\Big\{\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Big]\cdot s(V)\Big],

where the last equality is by
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}s(V)\big]=\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}\mathbb{E}\{s(V)\}=0
. Combining (
S45
), (
S46
), (
S47
) and (
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du-\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Big]s(V)\Bigg],

and therefore

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(x,z,m)+\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(x,z,m)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(x)du-\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Bigg\}

(S49)

is the EIF of
\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}
.

Step 2 (EIF of
\mathbb{E}[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du]
):
We obtain the following derivates,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu^{(\theta)}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu^{(\theta)}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big]\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

(S50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(X)\Big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mu^{(\theta)}_{1}(X,m_{1})\Big|_{\theta=0}-\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mu^{(\theta)}_{0}(X,m_{0})\Big|_{\theta=0}\Big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)\Big|_{\theta=0}du\Big].

Similar to (
), the first term on the right-hand side of (
S50
) equals to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\cdot s(V)\Big]

(S51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}\cdot s(V)\Big].

For the second term of (
S50
), we first derive

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\mu}^{(\theta)}_{z}(x,m_{z})\Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}(Y\mid X=x,Z=z,M=m_{z})\Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{Ys(Y\mid X=x,Z=z,M=m_{z})\mid X=x,Z=z,M=m_{z}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\mid X=x,Z=z,M=m_{z}\big\}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mu^{(\theta)}_{z}(X,m_{z})\Big|_{\theta=0}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}

(S52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\big\{Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\mid X,Z=z,M=m_{z}\big\}\cdot k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)dm_{1-z}dm_{z}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{(m_{1},m_{0})}(X)dm_{1-z}}_{=\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,M=m_{z})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\mid X,Z=z,M=m_{z}\big\}dm_{z}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,m_{z})}{f(M=m_{z}\mid X,Z=z)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\mid X,Z=z,M=m_{z}\big\}\cdot f(M=m_{z}\mid X,Z=z)dm_{z}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,M)}{f(M\mid X,Z=z)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{Ys(Y\mid X,Z=z,M)\mid X,Z=z,M\big\}\mid X,Z=z\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,M)}{f(M\mid X,Z=z)}\cdot Ys(Y\mid X,Z=z,M)\mid X,Z=z,M\Big\}\mid X,Z=z\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,M)}{f(M\mid X,Z=z)}\cdot Ys(Y\mid X,Z=z,M)\mid X,Z=z\Big]\Big]\quad(\textup{LOTE})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{{1}(Z=z)}{\pi_{z}(X)}\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{f(M\mid X,Z=z)}\cdot Ys(Y\mid X,Z=z,M)\mid X\Big\}\Big]\quad(\textup{LOTE})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{\mathbb{I}(Z=z)\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)f(M\mid X,Z)}\cdot Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\mid X\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{1(Z=z)\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)f_{ZM}(X)}\cdot Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\Big\}\quad\text{(LOTE)}.

By (
S52
), the second term on the right-hand side of (
S50
) is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{\frac{1(Z=1){\gamma_{1}}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)f_{ZM}(X)}Y-\frac{1(Z=0){\gamma_{1}}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)f_{ZM}(X)}Y\Big\}s(Y\mid X,Z,M)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}{\gamma_{1}}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)f_{ZM}(X)}Ys(Y\mid X,Z,M)\Big\},

recalling that

\xi_{1}(x,z,m)=\{\pi_{z}(x)f_{zm}(x)\}^{-1}{(-1)^{z+1}{\gamma_{1}}^{(z)}(x,m)}.

By Lemma
S2
with
A_{1}=Y
and
A_{2}=(X,Z,M)
, the second term on the right-hand side of (
S50
) then is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi_{1}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}\cdot s(V)\Big].

(S53)

where
\tilde{\xi}_{1}(X,Z,M,Y)={\xi}_{1}(X,Z,M,Y)-\mathbb{E}\{{\xi}_{1}(X,Z,M,Y)\mid X,Z,M\}
.

For the third term on the right-hand side of (
S50
), similar arguments to (
S46
) and (
S47
) can directly show

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)\Big|_{\theta=0}du\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\{\tilde{\xi}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(X,Z,M)\big\}\cdot s(V)\Big],

where recalling Section
S1
, we denote

(i)

\tilde{\xi}_{a}(X,Z,M)=\xi_{a}(X,Z,M)-\mathbb{E}\{\xi_{a}(X,Z,M)\mid X,Z\}
for
a=2,3
;

(ii)

\xi_{2}(X,Z,M)=\frac{1}{\pi_{Z}(X)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbb{I}(M\leq m_{Z})\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\}k_{u^{*}}(u)c_{\rho}^{(Z)}\big\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\big\}

\{\prod_{z\in\{0,1\}}f_{zm_{z}}(X)\}du
;

(iii)

\xi_{3}(X,Z,M)=\frac{1}{\pi_{Z}(X)}{\gamma}_{2}^{(Z)}(X,M)
;

(iv)

\gamma_{2}^{(1)}(X,M)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mu_{1}(X,M)-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\}k_{u^{*}}(M,m_{0})c_{\rho}\{F_{1M}(X),F_{0m_{0}}(X)\}f_{0m_{0}}(X)dm_{0}
;

(v)

\gamma_{2}^{(0)}(X,M)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,M)\}k_{u^{*}}(m_{1},M)c_{\rho}\{F_{1m_{1}}(X),F_{0M}(X)\}f_{1m_{1}}(X)dm_{1}
.

Combining (
S50
), (
S51
), (
S53
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu^{(\theta)}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu^{(\theta)}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big]\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\tilde{\xi}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(X,Z,M)+\xi_{1}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big]\cdot s(V)\Bigg].

Therefore the EIF of
\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]
is

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{2}(x,z,m)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(x,z,m)+\xi_{1}(x,z,m)\{y-\mu_{z}(x,m)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(x)du

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Bigg\}.

(S54)

Step 3 (EIF of
\tau_{u^{*}}
):
Recalling identification formula (
8
) and applying Lemma
S3
with derived EIFs in Step 1 and Step 2, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\tau_{u^{*},\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu^{(\theta)}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu^{(\theta)}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big]\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

(S55)

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]}{\big\{\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]\big\}^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e^{(\theta)}_{u}(X)du\Big\}\ \Big|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\tilde{\xi}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(X,Z,M)+\xi_{1}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{$\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du$}}-\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big]\cdot s(V)\Bigg]$}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]}{\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}\big]^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big\{\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}

\displaystyle\cdot s(V)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}}\cdot\Big[\tilde{\xi}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(X,Z,M)+\xi_{1}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big]}{\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}\big]^{2}}\cdot\Big\{\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{1}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Big]

\displaystyle\cdot s(V)\Bigg],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Bigg[\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}}\cdot\Big[\tilde{\xi}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(X,Z,M)+\xi_{1}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{\tau_{u^{*}}}{\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}\big]}\cdot\Big\{\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(X,Z,M)+\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(X,Z,M)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\Big\}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\cdot s(V)\Bigg],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\phi_{{\rm np}}(X,Z,M,Y)s(V)\Bigg],

after rearranging the terms and recalling the definitions in Section
S1
and (
7
). (
S55
) combining with (
S38
) proves that
\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)=\phi_{{\rm kn}}(x,z,m,y)+\tilde{\phi}_{\mathrm{np}}(x,z,m)
is the full EIF of
\tau_{u^{*}}
.

S8.3.3
Derivation of
\phi_{{\rm p}}(x,z,m,y)
under Scenario (ii)

Under the model assumption that
\mathcal{M}_{{\rm ps}}
is parametrically modeled and estimated, we first derive the corresponding tangent space
\Lambda
and then project
\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)
onto
\Lambda
to obtain the new EIF
\phi_{{\rm p}}(x,z,m,y)
under this specific scenario
[
Van der Vaart, 2000
,
\mathsection
25.3]
.

Consider any regular submodel with likelihood
f_{\theta,\beta}(V=v)
, parametrized with some one-dimensional parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and the principal score model parameter
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\beta}}
; the true model takes place when
f_{\theta=0,\beta=\beta^{*}}(v)=f(v)
. The likelihood is decomposed as

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\beta}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\theta}(X=x)f_{\theta}(Z=z\mid X=x)f(M=m\mid X=x,Z=z,\beta)\cdot f_{\theta}(Y=y\mid X=x,Z=z,M=m).

Thus the score function of submodel is,

\displaystyle s_{\beta^{*}}(v)=\begin{bmatrix}s(x)+s(z\mid x)+s(y\mid x,z,m)\\
s_{zm}(x\mid\beta^{*})\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{1+d_{\beta}},

where we recall
s(x)=\partial\log f_{\theta}(X=x)/\partial\theta\mid_{\theta=0}
,
s_{zm}(x\mid\beta^{*})=\partial\log f(M=m\mid X=x,Z=z,\beta)/\partial\beta\mid_{\beta=\beta^{*}}
, and similarly for others. The parametric submodel tangent space can be represented as

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\theta}=\underbrace{\Big\{\ell_{1}\{s(x)+s(z\mid x)+s(y\mid x,z,m)\}\mid\ell_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\Big\}}_{\Lambda_{1}}\oplus\underbrace{\Big\{\ell_{2}^{\top}s_{zm}(x\mid\beta^{*})\mid\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\beta}}\Big\}}_{\Lambda_{2}}.

One has
\Lambda_{1}\perp\Lambda_{2}
, as by LOTE,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\ell_{1}\{s(X)+s(Z\mid X)+s(Y\mid X,Z,M)\}\cdot\ell_{2}^{\top}s_{ZM}(X\mid\beta^{*})\Big]

(S56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\ell_{1}\{s(X)+s(Z\mid X)\}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{\ell_{2}^{\top}s_{ZM}(X\mid\beta^{*})\mid X,Z\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big[\ell_{2}^{\top}s_{ZM}(X\mid\beta^{*})\cdot\mathbb{E}\big\{s(Y\mid X,Z,M)\mid X,Z,M\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

for any
\ell_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\beta}}
. Then the semiparametric tangent space
\Lambda
is the mean-square closure of
\Lambda_{\theta}
, over all possible regular submodels associated with parameter
\theta
. Note
\beta
is associated with a known and fixed model, namely
f_{zm}(x\mid\beta)
. Thus we have
\Lambda=\bar{\Lambda}_{1}+\Lambda_{2}
, where
\bar{\Lambda}_{1}
is the mean-squre closure of
{\Lambda}_{1}
over all regular parametric submodels associated with
\theta
. Recalling (
S61
)–(
S62
), we similarly have
\bar{\Lambda}_{1}=H_{1}\oplus H_{2}\oplus H_{4}
and thus

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{1}\oplus H_{2}\oplus H_{4}\oplus\Lambda_{2},

(S57)

where

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle=\{h(x)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X)\}=0\},

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle=\{h(x,z)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X,Z)\mid X\}=0\},

\displaystyle H_{4}

\displaystyle=\{h(x,z,m,y)\mid\mathbb{E}\{h(X,Z,M,Y)\mid X,Z,M\}=0\},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\ell_{2}^{\top}s_{zm}(x\mid\beta^{*})\mid\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\beta}}\Big\}.

Similar to (
S56
), we can check that

\displaystyle H_{1}\perp H_{2}\perp H_{4}\perp\Lambda_{2}.

(S58)

To derive the semiparametric EIF, we need to obtain the projection of
\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)
onto the semiparametric tangent space
\Lambda
. We first decompose
\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)
in (
S55
) as follows,

\displaystyle\phi_{{\rm np}}(x,z,m,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\xi}_{2}(x,z,m)+\tilde{\xi}_{3}(x,z,m)-\tau_{u^{*}}\big\{\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{2}(x,z,m)+\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{3}(x,z,m)\big\}}{\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}}$}}}_{\phi_{1}(x,z,m)}+\underbrace{\frac{{\xi}_{1}(x,z,m)\{y-\mu_{z}(x,m)\}}{\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}}}_{\phi_{2}(x,z,m,y)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\{\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(x,m_{0})\}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(x)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(x)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}}$}}}_{\phi_{3}(x)}.

Since the orthogonal projection is a linear operator, we only need to derive the projections of
\phi_{1}
–
\phi_{3}
onto
\Lambda
, respectively, and sum them up to get the final projections. By definition, one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\phi_{2}(X,Z,M,Y)\mid X,Z,M\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{k}^{-1}\Big[\xi_{1}(X,Z,M)\big\{\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X,Z,M)-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\big\}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

recalling
\xi_{1}
is defined in Section
S1
; we denote
c_{k}=\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\}
for simplicity. Therefore
\phi_{2}(x,z,m,y)\in H_{4}
, and by (
S58
), we have the projection of
\phi_{2}(x,z,m,y)
onto
\Lambda
is itself. Similarly, we can check that
\mathbb{E}\{\phi_{3}(X)\}=\tau_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}}=0
by Theorem
2
. Thus
\phi_{3}(x)\in H_{1}
. Similarly, the projection of
\phi_{3}(x)
onto
\Lambda
is also itself.

It is left to find the projection of
\phi_{1}(x,z,m)
. Recalling the definitions of
\tilde{\xi}_{2},\tilde{\xi}_{3},\tilde{\xi}_{2}^{\prime},\tilde{\xi}_{3}^{\prime}
in Section
S1
, one can easily show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\phi_{1}(X,Z,M)\mid X,Z\}=0.

We claim that the projections of
\phi_{1}(X,Z,M)
onto
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
, and
H_{4}
are all zero, because
\phi_{1}(X,Z,M)\perp H_{j}
,
j=1,2,4
