\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\,\,\quad{f}_{1m_{1}}(X\mid{\beta}^{*}){f}_{0m_{0}}(X\mid{\beta}^{*})\big\{F^{\prime}_{zm_{z}}(X\mid{\beta}^{*})\big\}^{\top}du\Bigg]

\displaystyle\cdot(\hat{\beta}-\beta^{*})

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.8}{\mbox{$\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1,2,3}\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\Big[\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}}{\bar{\pi}_{Z}(X)}\big\{Y-\bar{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\big\}\Big]\delta_{X,Z,M}^{(j)}\Bigg](\hat{\beta}-\beta^{*})$}}.

S8.6.3
Putting all pieces together

Combining (
S67
), (
S76
) and (
S102
), we have that, in general,

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}}

(S103)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\Bigg[\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big]}{\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}\big]^{2}}\Bigg[(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\Big\}+\mathcal{A}_{1}(\hat{\beta}-\beta^{*})\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du\big\}}(\hat{\beta}-\beta^{*})+\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}r_{\pi}r_{\mu})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\Bigg[\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}(\hat{\beta}-\beta^{*})+\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}r_{\pi}r_{\mu})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(h^{-1}n^{-1/2}+h^{-1}r_{\pi}r_{\mu}\big),

(S104)

where the the second equality is by Theorem
4
under Assumption
4
(1); The first two terms in (
S103
) are
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}n^{-1/2})
with a standard argument similar as before. We note that when
h
is fixed, we have
\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}>0
is fixed, and if
h\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{k_{u^{*}}(U)\mid X\Big\}\Big]

(S105)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[k_{u^{*}}(U)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{u^{*}}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})\succsim 1

by (
S26
), which is also bounded away from
0
. We define
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
in (
S1
) and (

),
\delta_{X,1,M}^{(1)}
–
\delta_{X,1,M}^{(3)}
in (
S6
), and
\delta_{X,0,M}^{(1)}
–
\delta_{X,0,M}^{(3)}
in (

).

In the following, we focus on the fixed-
h
setting for the asymptotic analysis of Loc.PCE. Then (
S104
) becomes

\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(n^{-1/2}+r_{\pi}r_{\mu}\big),

which implies (
12
). If moreover,
r_{\pi}r_{\mu}=o(n^{-1/2})
and
\hat{\beta}
is asymptotically linear, we further have,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}})

(S106)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\Bigg[\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}(\hat{\beta}-\beta^{*})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\Bigg[\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\psi(X,Z,M)\Bigg]+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\leadsto

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}_{u^{*}}^{2})

(S107)

where the second equality is by that
\psi(X,Z,M)
is mean-zero and
\|\{\mathcal{A}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}\}[\mathbb{E}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}

(u){e}_{u}(X)du\}]^{-1}\|
is bounded by some constant, which can be easily shown with a similar argument as the previous proof. Finally, (
S107
) follows from the central limit theorem (CLT) and Slutsky’s theorem, as the random variable in
\sqrt{n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})[\,\cdot\,]
in (
S106
) is mean-zero and has bounded variance under the conditions of Theorem
4
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}_{u^{*}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Var}\Bigg[\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\psi(X,Z,M)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\psi(X,Z,M)+\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\Bigg]^{2}.

In Theorem
3
(ii), we have derived the semiparametric EIF under the setting that the parametric model
\mathcal{M}_{{\rm ps}}
in (
9
) is specified and
\hat{\beta}
is estimated. By the classic semiparametric theory (see e.g.
Bickel et al. [1993]
), the corresponding semiparametric efficiency bound is

\displaystyle\sigma_{u^{*},\text{eff}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\phi^{2}_{{\rm p}}(X,Z,M,Y)\}

(S108)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du-\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{A}^{*}_{2}-\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}^{*}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}I_{\beta^{*}}^{-1}s_{ZM}(X\mid\beta^{*})\Bigg]^{2}.

We now show that, under the setting of Theorem
5
, the asymptotic variance of our proposed estimator given in (
S5
) achieves such bound and thus is semeparaetric efficient. Under the conditions of Theorem
5
(III), we can check the following facts through definitions:

(i)

\bar{\tilde{\xi}}_{1}=\tilde{\xi}_{1}
because
\bar{\mu}=\mu
,
\bar{\pi}=\pi
;

(ii)

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{1}^{*}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\mathcal{A}_{2}^{*}
, noting that the third term of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
in (
S2
) under our scenario becomes,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2,3}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}}{{\pi}_{Z}(X)}\big\{Y-{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\big\}\Big]\delta_{X,Z,M}^{(j)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2,3}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}}{{\pi}_{Z}(X)}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\big\{Y-{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\mid X,Z,M\big\}}_{=0}\Big]\delta_{X,Z,M}^{(j)}\Bigg]=0;

(iii)

\psi(X,Z,M)=I_{\beta^{*}}^{-1}s_{ZM}(X\mid\beta^{*})
by
\psi(x,z,m)=I_{\beta^{*}}^{-1}s_{zm}(x\mid\beta^{*})
.

Based on above facts and comparing (
S5
) with (
S108
), we can observe that
\bar{\sigma}_{u^{*}}^{2}=\sigma_{u^{*},\text{eff}}^{2}
, which implies that
\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}
is semiparametric efficient.

S8.7
Proof of Theorem
6

Combining (
S104
) and Proposition
1
, we have,

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}})+(\tau_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(h^{-1}n^{-1/2}+h^{-1}r_{\pi}r_{\mu}+h^{2}\big).

If
r_{\pi}r_{\mu}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
, one further has
\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}n^{-1/2}+h^{2})
. When choosing
h\asymp n^{-1/6}
, we then deduce the minimax optimal convergence rate
\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/3})
.

By (
S103
) and Proposition
1
, when
h=o(n^{-1/6})
,
r_{\mu}r_{\pi}=o(n^{-1/2})
and
\hat{\beta}
is asymptotically linear, we further have,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}h(\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}})

(S110)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}h(\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau_{u^{*}})+\sqrt{n}h({\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\Bigg[\frac{h\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}+\frac{h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}-\frac{h\tau_{u^{*}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)e_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}

\displaystyle+\frac{h\mathcal{A}_{2}-h\tau_{u^{*}}\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\psi(X,Z,M)\Bigg]+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{n}r_{\pi}r_{\mu})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n}h^{3})}_{o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)}.

To apply central limit theorem, under regularity conditions, we check the
L_{2}
-norm bounds of all terms in (
) and (
S110
). For example, for the first term in (
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{h\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\Bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{h^{2}\{\bar{\pi}_{Z}(X)f_{ZM}(X)\}^{-2}\big\{{{\gamma_{1}}^{(Z)}(Z,M)}\big\}^{2}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}^{2}}{\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}\big]^{2}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[{\big\{h{{\gamma_{1}}^{(Z)}(Z,M)}\big\}^{2}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[{\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}^{2}}\Big]=\mathcal{O}(1),

where the first inequality is by (
S105
), Assumption
S8
and Assumption
S13
, and the second inequality is by
\sup_{z,m}h{{\gamma_{1}}^{(z)}(z,m)}=\mathcal{O}(1)
under Assumption
S9
. For the second term in (
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big\{\bar{\mu}_{1}(X,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{0}(X,m_{0})\big\}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du}{\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}}\Bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[{h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)du}\Bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{2}\rightarrow 0.

Thus the second term in (
) is negligible, i.e., it does not contribute to the asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{n}h(\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}})
. We can similarly analyze all terms in (
) and (
S110
) and show all terms have either bounded or vanishing
L_{2}
norms; We omit details for simplicity. Finally by CLT, we can finally show that

\sqrt{n}h(\hat{\tau}_{u^{*}}-\tau^{*}_{u^{*}})\leadsto\mathcal{N}(0,\tilde{\sigma}_{u^{*}}^{2}),

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{u^{*}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{C}\psi(X,Z,M)+h\bar{{\xi}}_{1}\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\}\big]^{2}}{\big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u){e}_{u}(X)du\big\}\big]^{2}},

(S111)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2,3}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Big[\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}}{\bar{\pi}_{Z}(X)}\big\{Y-\bar{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\big\}\Big]h\delta_{X,Z,M}^{(j)}\Bigg].

\Box

S8.8
Proof of Theorem
S7
.

We focus on

\displaystyle\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du

(S112)

as an example to investigate the computational complexity bound of calculating it. The argument is similar to derive the computational complexity in approximating
\llbracket\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},{\bm{\cdot}})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},{\scriptstyle\star})\}\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\rrbracket_{u^{*}}
. We first decompose the domain of the integration in (
S112
), to a 2D-square centered at
u^{*}
with an
C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}
edge length for some
C_{1}>0
, and the rest of the area in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We denote the 2D domain by
\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}
. Then (
S112
) can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du

(S113)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du.

Recall that
\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})
is uniformly bounded for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
\Psi(\cdot)
be the Gaussian CDF. Then by the tail bound of
\Psi(\cdot)
(see e.g.,
Vershynin [2018]
), we have for some sufficiently large and fixed
C_{1}>0
,
1-\Psi(C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2)=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
, and thus

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\left\{2\Psi\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}}{2}\right)-1\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1-(1-n^{-1})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1}).

Thus we have

\displaystyle\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1}),

and to approximate
\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}
with an
o(n^{-1/2})
accuracy, it is sufficient to numerically approximate the first term on the right-hand side of (
S113
). We can further write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}}\hat{e}_{u}(X_{i})\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{m_{0}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{0}^{C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}\int_{0}^{C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}}\hat{e}_{t_{1}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{1}^{*},t_{0}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{0}^{*}}(X_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\cdot\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{t_{1}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2}{h},\frac{t_{0}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2}{h}\right)dt_{1}t_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}^{2}\log n\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\hat{e}_{C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{1}^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{0}^{*}}(X_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\cdot\mathcal{K}\left({C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2},{C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2}\right)d{t_{1}}dt_{0}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle C_{1}^{2}\log n\cdot\mathcal{I}_{i}.

Denote the following
N\times N
uniform grids on
[0,1]^{2}
:
\mathcal{H}_{N}=\{(h_{1}/N,h_{0}/N)\mid(h_{1},h_{0})\in[N]^{2}\}
. We will approximate
\mathcal{I}_{i}
by the following Riemann sum,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{i}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{(t_{1},t_{0})\in\mathcal{H}_{N}}\hat{e}_{C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{1}^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{0}^{*}}(X_{i})

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{K}\left({C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2},{C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2}\right),

which has a computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}(N^{2}r_{n})
, recalling that calculating

\hat{e}_{C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{1}^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{0}^{*}}(X_{i})

is
\mathcal{O}(r_{n})
, and calculating
\mathcal{K}\left({C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2},{C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2}\right)
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
for the standard Gaussian kernel
\mathcal{K}
. We need the following direct deduction of the main theorem in
Davis and Rabinowitz [2007,

§

5.5]
to bound the approximation error.

Lemma S5
(
Davis and Rabinowitz [2007]
)

Suppose
f(t_{1},t_{0})
satisfies that

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial^{2}f(m_{1},m_{0})}{\partial m_{1}\partial m_{0}}\right|dm_{1}dm_{0}+\int_{[0,1]}\left|\frac{\partial f(m_{1},1)}{\partial m_{1}}\right|dm_{1}+\int_{[0,1]}\left|\frac{\partial f(1,m_{0})}{\partial m_{0}}\right|dm_{0}\leq V,

(S114)

for some
V>0
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{(t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{0})\in\mathcal{H}_{N}}f(t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{0})-\int_{[0,1]^{2}}f(t_{1},t_{0})dt_{1}dt_{0}\right|\leq\frac{2V}{{N}}.

Now let

\displaystyle f(t_{1},t_{0})

\displaystyle=\hat{e}_{C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{1}^{*},C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}h\sqrt{\log n}/2+m_{0}^{*}}(X_{i})

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{K}\left({C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2},{C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}t_{0}-C_{1}\sqrt{\log n}/2}\right).

Under (
S15
) and with a standard Gaussian kernel
\mathcal{K}
, it can be shown by some direct calculations that, for any
C_{1}
,
h
and
n
, condition (
S114
) is satisfied with
V=C_{V}
, for some constant
C_{V}>0
only depending on
C
in (
S15
). Then by Lemma
S5
with
N=(\log n)^{1+\epsilon/2}\sqrt{n}
, for any fixed
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle|C_{1}^{2}\log n\cdot\mathcal{I}_{i}-C_{1}^{2}\log n\cdot\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{i}|=\mathcal{O}\left\{(\log n)^{-\epsilon/2}n^{-1/2}\right\}=o(n^{-1/2}).

In summary,
C_{1}^{2}\log n\cdot\bar{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
is a numerical approximation of
\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}
with an approximation error bounded by
o(n^{-1/2})
, and it has a computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}(N^{2}r_{n})=\mathcal{O}\{(\log n)^{2+\epsilon}nr_{n}\}
. Similar result also holds for
\llbracket\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},{\bm{\cdot}})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},{\scriptstyle\star})\}\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\rrbracket_{u^{*}}
. Thus, to numerically approximate all
\{\llbracket\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{\llbracket\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(X_{i},{\bm{\cdot}})-\hat{\mu}_{0}(X_{i},{\scriptstyle\star})\}\hat{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X_{i})\rrbracket_{u^{*}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, with approximation errors bounded by
o(n^{-1/2})
uniformly for all the corresponding numerical integrations, the overall computational complexity can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}\{(\log n)^{2+\epsilon}n^{2}r_{n}\}
.

Next, we study the numerical integration for
\hat{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(Z_{i})}(M_{i},X_{i})
. Without loss of generality, assume
Z_{i}=0
, and then

\hat{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(Z_{i})}(M_{i},X_{i})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(m_{1},M_{i})\hat{e}_{(m_{1},M_{i})}(X_{i})dm_{1}=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{M_{i}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)\hat{e}_{(m_{1},M_{i})}(X_{i})dm_{1}.

Following a similar argument as before, we only need to focus on the numerical approximation for

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{m_{1}^{*}-C_{2}h\sqrt{\log(n/h)}}^{m_{1}^{*}+C_{2}h\sqrt{\log(n/h)}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{M_{i}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)\hat{e}_{(m_{1},M_{i})}(X_{i})dm_{1},

for some sufficiently large but fixed
C_{2}>0
. Since
h\succsim n^{-1/2}
, we further have that we only need to focus on the numerical approximation for

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{m_{1}^{*}-C_{3}h\sqrt{\log n}}^{m_{1}^{*}+C_{3}h\sqrt{\log n}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{m_{1}-m_{1}^{*}}{h},\frac{M_{i}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)\hat{e}_{(m_{1},M_{i})}(X_{i})dm_{1}

(S115)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}{h}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathcal{K}\left(C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}t_{1},\frac{M_{i}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)\hat{e}_{(C_{3}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}+m_{1}^{*},M_{i})}(X_{i})dt_{1}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\frac{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}{h}{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}},

(S116)

for some sufficiently large
C_{3}>0
. Let

f(t_{1})=\mathcal{K}\left(C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}t_{1},\frac{M_{i}-m_{0}^{*}}{h}\right)\hat{e}_{(C_{3}h\sqrt{\log n}t_{1}+m_{1}^{*},M_{i})}(X_{i}),

be the integrand of
\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}
. It is easy to verify that under the regularity conditions, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d}{dt_{1}}f(t_{1})\right|=\mathcal{O}\ (\sqrt{\log n}),

uniformly for all
t_{1}\in[-1,1]
. To approximate
\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}=\int_{-1}^{1}f(t_{1})dt_{1}
, we use the following Riemann sum:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t_{1}=-1+\frac{2h}{N},\,h\in[N]}f(t_{1}),

which has a computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}(Nr_{n})
, where similarly as (
S8.8
), the computational complexity to calculate each
f(t_{1})
is
\mathcal{O}(r_{n})
. By the result from
Davis and Rabinowitz [2007,

§

2.1]
which provides the error bound for the 1D numerical integration, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}{h}{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}-\frac{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}{h}\bar{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\log n}}{hN}\right).

By taking
N=(\log n)^{1+\epsilon}{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}{h}{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}-\frac{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}{h}\bar{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}\right|=o(n^{-1/2}),

recalling that
h\succsim n^{-1/2}
. Thus we can use
{C_{3}\sqrt{\log n}}\bar{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}/{h}
to approximate
\hat{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(Z_{i})}(M_{i},X_{i})
with computational complexity bounded by
\mathcal{O}\{(\log n)^{1+\epsilon}{n}r_{n}\}
. To numerically approximate all
\{\hat{\gamma}_{1,u^{*}}^{(Z_{i})}(M_{i},X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, with approximation errors bounded by
o(n^{-1/2})
uniformly for all corresponding numerical integrations, the overall computational complexity can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}\{(\log n)^{1+\epsilon}n^{2}r_{n}\}
.

\Box

S8.9
Proof of Theorem
S8
.

When
(\bar{\pi},\bar{e})=(\pi,e)
, we have
\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\bar{\tau}_{u^{*}}
, and by Theorem
4
, we have
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\bar{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\tau^{*}_{u^{*}}
. When
\bar{\mu}=\mu
, we can show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\check{\xi}_{1,u^{*}}(X,Z,M)\{Y-\bar{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\check{\xi}_{1,u^{*}}(X,Z,M)\mathbb{E}\{Y-{\mu}_{Z}(X,M)\mid X,Z,M\}\Big]=0,

which implies that

\displaystyle\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\big\{\mu_{1}(X,{\bm{\cdot}})-\mu_{0}(X,{\scriptstyle\star})\big\}\bar{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X)\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}}{\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\bar{e}}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X)\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}}.

Since
\bar{e}_{u}(x)
satisfies the same regularity condition as
{e}_{u}(x)
, similar to the proof of Proposition
1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket\big\{\mu_{1}(X,{\bm{\cdot}})-\mu_{0}(X,{\scriptstyle\star})\big\}\bar{e}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X)\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}}{\mathbb{E}\big\llbracket{\bar{e}}_{{\bm{\cdot}}{\scriptstyle\star}}(X)\big\rrbracket_{u^{*}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\big\{\mu_{1}(X,m^{*}_{1})-\mu_{0}(X,m^{*}_{0})\big\}\bar{e}_{u^{*}}(X)\big]}{\bar{e}_{u^{*}}},

where we define
\bar{e}_{u^{*}}=\mathbb{E}\{\bar{e}_{u^{*}}(X)\}
. We have
\bar{e}_{u^{*}}=\mathbb{E}\{\bar{e}_{u^{*}}(X)\}>0
by the regularity conditions in Proposition
1
which now apply for
\bar{e}_{u}(x)
. Since
\mu_{z}(x,m)
follows the linear model, we further have

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\{\lambda_{d_{X}+2}+\lambda_{d_{X}+3}(m_{1}^{*}-m_{0}^{*})\}\bar{e}_{u^{*}}(X)\right]}{\bar{e}_{u^{*}}}=\lambda_{d_{X}+2}+\lambda_{d_{X}+3}(m_{1}^{*}-m_{0}^{*}).

By Theorem
1
, we can also show

\tau_{u^{*}}^{*}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\big\{\lambda_{d_{X}+2}+\lambda_{d_{X}+3}(m_{1}^{*}-m_{0}^{*})\big\}e_{u^{*}}(X)\big]}{e_{u^{*}}}=\lambda_{d_{X}+2}+\lambda_{d_{X}+3}(m_{1}^{*}-m_{0}^{*}),

and thus
\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\check{\tau}_{u^{*}}=\tau_{u^{*}}^{*}
.

\Box

S9
Proof of Lemma
S4

S9.1
Preliminary

We first introduce some basic concepts and results in the empirical process theory, e.g., the stochastic equicontinuity, covering number, entropy integral; See
Vaart and Wellner [1996]
for a more detailed introduction. Based on the application of empirical process theory, we prove Lemma
S4
. Denote
\mathbb{G}_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})
. Let
\mathcal{E}(g)
be an operator of function
g
where
g
is in some metric space
\mathcal{G}
equipped with semimetric
d(\cdot,\cdot)
. In this paper, we typically consider the uniform difference metric, i.e., for any
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle d(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathbb{V}}|g_{1}(v)-g_{2}(v)|.

We now introduce some notations to measure the complexity of the functional space
\mathcal{G}
. Let
G
be an envelope function for
\mathcal{G}
such that
G(v)\geq|g(v)|
for any
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
v\in\mathbb{V}
. Let
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|)
denote the covering number of
\mathcal{G}
, which is the minimal number of
\epsilon
-balls needed to cover
\mathcal{G}
; the
\epsilon
-ball is under the distance induced by norm
\|\cdot\|
. We then define the uniform entropy integral,

\displaystyle J(\delta,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log N\{\epsilon\|G\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}}d\epsilon,

where
Q
can be any random measure,
\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}
is the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
-norm under
Q
, such that
\|g\|_{Q,2}=\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{V}}g^{2}dQ}
.
A series of empirical process
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}(g)\mid n\geq 1,\,g\in\mathcal{G}\}
is
stochastically equicontinuous
if for every
\epsilon,\zeta>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle{\lim\sup}_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{P}\Bigg\{\sup_{d(g_{1},g_{2})<\delta\atop g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}}\Big|\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}(g_{1})-\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}(g_{2})\Big|>\epsilon\Bigg\}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\zeta.

Above property implies that for any stochastically equicontinuous
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}(g)\mid n\geq 1,\,g\in\mathcal{G}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{n}\{\mathcal{E}(g_{1})-\mathcal{E}(g_{2})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(S117)

whenever
d(g_{1},g_{2})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}

[
Pollard, 2012
]
.

We also denote

\|\mathcal{E}\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\mathcal{E}\{g(V)\}|.

It can be seen that
\|\mathcal{E}\|_{\mathcal{G}}
is generally a random variable as it depends on
V
. To show a series of empirical process
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}(g)\mid n\geq 1,g\in\mathcal{G}\}
is stochastically equicontinuous, one can use Theorem 2.11.1 in
Vaart and Wellner [1996]
, which boils down the verification of stochastic equicontinuity, to checking the following two Lindeberg conditions and one condition of
\mathcal{G}
’s complexity,

(1).

\mathbb{E}\big\{\|\mathcal{E}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\cdot 1\big(\|\mathcal{E}\|_{\mathcal{G}}>\epsilon\sqrt{n}\big)\big\}\rightarrow 0
for every
\epsilon>0
,

(2).

\sup_{d(g_{1},g_{2})<\delta_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[\{\mathcal{E}(g_{1})-\mathcal{E}(g_{2})\}^{2}\big]\rightarrow 0
for every sequence
\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
,

(3).

\int_{0}^{\delta_{n}}\sqrt{\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{P}_{n},2})}d\epsilon\rightarrow 0
in probability, for every sequence
\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
.

Now we prove Lemma
S4
. Using the new notation, we decompose our target term to bound, namely
(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\{h(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})-h(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\}
, as

\displaystyle(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\{h(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})-h(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{-1}n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{1}(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})-h_{1}(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\}

(S118)

\displaystyle+n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{2}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{2}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}

\displaystyle+n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{3}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{3}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\},

where we define

\displaystyle h_{1}(V\mid\pi,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}h\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)f_{ZM}(X)}\big\{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)\big\},

\displaystyle h_{2}(V\mid\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})e_{u}(X)du,

\displaystyle h_{3}(V\mid\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\mu_{0}(X,m_{0})e_{u}(X)du.

In the following sections, we show the root-
n
-rates of all three terms on the right-hand side of (
S118
), and thereby show the desired result (
S98
).

S9.2
Bounding
\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{1}(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})-h_{1}(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\}

We use a similar proof strategy as
Kennedy et al. [2017, Appendix 5]
to bound the first term of (
S118
). Denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{Z+1}{h}\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{f_{ZM}(X)}g(V).

The following lemma gives a sufficient condition, under which
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g)\mid n\geq 1\}
is stochastically equicontinuous.

Lemma S6

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
4
and other regularity conditions in Section
S8.5
hold. Suppose
g
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
, such that
\mathcal{G}
has:

(i).

A finite uniform entropy integral, i.e.,
J(\delta,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{L}_{2})<+\infty
for every
\delta>0
;

(ii).

A uniformly bounded envelope function
G(v)
such that
\sup_{v\in\mathbb{V}}G(v)<C_{G}
with some universal constant
C_{G}>0
, and
G(v)>|g(v)|
for any
g\in\mathcal{G}
and any
v\in\mathbb{V}
.

Then
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g)\mid n\geq 1\}
is stochastically equicontinuous.

Proof of Lemma
S6
.
To show
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g)\mid n\geq 1\}
is stochastically equicontinuous, we only need to verify aforementioned Conditions (1)–(3) in Section
S9.1
. We first verify Lindeberg Condition (1). We have for any
c>0
,

\displaystyle\ \lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\text{pr}\Big\{\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}\cdot 1\big(\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{G}}>\epsilon\sqrt{n}\big)\geq c\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\text{pr}\Big(\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{G}}>c\sqrt{n}\Big)

(S119)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\text{pr}\Bigg\{\frac{{h}\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{f_{ZM}(X)}G(V)>c\sqrt{n}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where the last equality is for some universal constant
C_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}>0
, we have a.s.,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{{h}\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{f_{ZM}(X)}G(V)\Bigg|<C_{\mathcal{E}_{1}},

where
G(V)
is bounded by
C_{G}
,
f_{ZM}(X)
is bounded away from
0
under Assumption
S13
, and when
Z=0
,

\displaystyle{h}\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{h}\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(m_{1},M)e_{(m_{1},M)}(X)dm_{1}

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle{h}\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(m_{1},M)dm_{1}\quad\text{(Assumptions \ref{am:eux} and \ref{am:unibound})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{m_{0}\in\mathbb{R},h>0}h\int_{\mathbb{R}}k_{u^{*}}(m_{1},m_{0})dm_{1}

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle 1\quad(\text{Assumption \ref{am:kernel}}),

similar for
Z=1
and thus
h\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)
is uniformly bounded by some constant. By (
S119
),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}\cdot 1\big(\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{G}}>\epsilon\sqrt{n}\big)=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(S120)

On the other hand, under the regularity conditions,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg\{\frac{h\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)}{f_{ZM}(X)}G(V)\Bigg\}^{2}

(S121)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{G}^{2}c_{0}^{-2}h^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big\{\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{z=0,1}C_{G}^{2}c_{0}^{-2}h^{2}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\pi_{z}(X)\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,M)\Big\}^{2}\mid X,Z=z\Big]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle 1,

where the last inequality follows because we have when
z=0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\pi_{z}(X)\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{\gamma_{1}^{(z)}(X,M)\Big\}^{2}\mid X,Z=z\Big]\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\{\gamma_{1}^{(0)}(X,M)\Big\}^{2}\mid X,Z=0\Big]\Bigg]

(S122)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}k^{2}_{u^{*}}(m_{1},M)e_{(m_{1},M)}^{2}(X)dm_{1}\mid X,Z=0\Big\}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}k^{2}_{u^{*}}(m_{1},M)dm_{1}\mid X,Z=0\Big\}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k^{2}_{u^{*}}(u)f_{0m_{0}}(X)du\Bigg]

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle h^{-2},

where the third inequality is because that
e_{u}(x)
is uniformly bounded under regularity conditions and the last inequality is by
h^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}^{2}(u)du\precsim 1
and
f_{0m_{0}}(x)
is uniformly upper bounded; Similar result also holds for
z=1
. Combining (
S120
) and (
S122
), and noting
\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}\cdot 1\big(\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{G}}>\epsilon\sqrt{n}\big)\leq\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}
, we can invoke dominated convergence theorem and show Lindeberg Condition (1) holds for
\mathcal{E}_{1}(g)
.

We next verify Lindeberg Condition (2) when
d(g_{1},g_{2})=\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{\infty}
, where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is the sup-norm. We have,

\displaystyle\sup_{d(g_{1},g_{2})<\delta_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(g_{1})-\mathcal{E}_{1}(g_{2})\}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{\{h\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)\}^{2}}{f^{2}_{ZM}(X)}\Big\{g_{1}(V)-g_{2}(V)\Big\}^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{0}^{-2}h^{2}\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\{\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)\}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\precsim

\displaystyle\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0,

where the last inequality follows from
\|g_{1}-g_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta_{n}
and
\mathbb{E}\big[\{\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)\}^{2}\big]\precsim h^{-2}
as shown in (
S121
).

We finally verify Condition (3). By
Vaart and Wellner [1996,

§

2.11.1.1 & Lemma 2.11.6]
, Condition (3) is satisfied if
\mathcal{G}
has a finite uniform entropy integral and for some random measure
\nu_{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\Big\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(g_{1})-\mathcal{E}_{1}(g_{2})\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{V}}\big\{g_{1}(v)-g_{2}(v)\big\}^{2}d\nu_{n};

(S123)

see also the proof of Lemma 1 in
Kennedy et al. [2017]
. Noticing that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\Big\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(g_{1})-\mathcal{E}_{1}(g_{2})\Big\}^{2}=\frac{\{h\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)\}^{2}}{nf^{2}_{ZM}(X)}\Big\{g_{1}(V)-g_{2}(V)\Big\}^{2},

(
S123
) is satisfied by the following random measure

\nu_{n}=\frac{\{h\gamma_{1}^{(Z)}(X,M)\}^{2}}{nf^{2}_{ZM}(X)}\delta_{V},

where
\delta_{V}
is a (random) Dirac measure centered at
V
. We thus have verified Condition (3). With Conditions (1)–(3) being satisfied, we have
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g)\mid n\geq 1\}
is stochastically equicontinuous.

\Box

Define

g^{\natural}(V\mid\pi,\mu)=\frac{Y-\mu_{Z}(X,M)}{\pi_{Z}(X)}.

We then have

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{1}(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})-h_{1}(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{n}\Big[\mathcal{E}_{1}\big\{g^{\natural}(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})\big\}-\mathcal{E}_{1}\big\{g^{\natural}(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu}\big\}\Big].

(S124)

For the series of empirical process
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g^{\natural})\mid n\geq 1,\,g^{\natural}\in\mathcal{G}^{\natural}\}
where
g^{\natural}
can be all possible
g^{\natural}(\cdot\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})
and
g^{\natural}(\cdot\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})
under Assumption
4
. We first prove that there exists some metric space
\mathcal{G}^{\natural}
satisfying the condition in Lemma
S6
, and thus
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g^{\natural})\mid n\geq 1,\,g^{\natural}\in\mathcal{G}^{\natural}\}
is stochastically equicontinuous. Under Assumption
S13
, we have functional classes
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
containing
\bar{\mu},\hat{\mu}
and
\bar{\pi},\bar{\pi}
, respectively, and both
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
have uniformly bounded envelop functions and finite uniform entropy integrals. We then construct

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\natural}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Y}\ominus\mathcal{G}_{\mu})\circ\mathcal{G}_{\pi}^{-1},

where we denote
\mathcal{Y}
is the set of a single function outputting
Y
from
Z
, and for two functional sets, e.g.,
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we define the operations,
\mathcal{G}\ominus\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\{g-g^{\prime}\mid g\in\mathcal{G},g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\}
,
\mathcal{G}^{-1}=\{g^{-1}\mid g\in\mathcal{G}\}
, and
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\{gg^{\prime}\mid g\in\mathcal{G},g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\}
. Under Assumptions
S12
and
S13
such that
\mu_{z}(x,m)
and
\pi_{z}(x)^{-1}
are uniformly bounded by some constant and
\mathbb{Y}
is compact, it can be seen that
\mathcal{G}^{\natural}
has an envelope function with a uniform constant upper bound; thus Condition (ii) in Lemma
S6
is verified. The Condition (i) in Lemma
S6
follows from the basic property of uniform entropy integral. Under Assumption
S13
, both
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}^{-1}
have bounded entropy integrals and bounded envelop functions and so does the single-function space
\mathcal{Y}
. Therefore, by
Andrews [1994, Theorem 3]
, one has the uniform entropy integral of
\mathcal{G}^{\natural}
is bounded. Thus Lemma
S6
verifies
\{\mathbb{G}_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(g^{\natural})\mid n\geq 1,\,g^{\natural}\in\mathcal{G}^{\natural}\}
is stochastically equicontinuous.

On the other hand, recalling we define
d(\cdot,\cdot)
as the uniform difference metric, we have

\displaystyle d\Big\{g^{\natural}(\cdot\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu}),g^{\natural}(\cdot\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathbb{V}}\Big|\frac{y-\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)}{\hat{\pi}_{z}(x)}-\frac{y-\bar{\mu}_{z}(x,m)}{\bar{\pi}_{z}(x)}\Big|

(S125)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in\mathbb{V}}\Big|\frac{y}{\hat{\pi}_{z}(x)\bar{\pi}_{z}(x)}\{\bar{\pi}_{z}(x)-\hat{\pi}_{z}(x)\}\Big|+\sup_{v\in\mathbb{V}}\Big|\frac{1}{\hat{\pi}_{z}(x)}\{\hat{\mu}_{z}(x,m)-\bar{\mu}_{z}(x,m)\}\Big|

\displaystyle+\sup_{v\in\mathbb{V}}\Big|\frac{\bar{\mu}_{z}(x,m)}{\hat{\pi}_{z}(x)\bar{\pi}_{z}(x)}\{\bar{\pi}_{z}(x)-\hat{\pi}_{z}(x)\}\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{\mathbb{P}}(\|\hat{\pi}-\bar{\pi}\|_{\infty}+\|\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}\|_{\infty})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

under Assumptions
S12
and
S13
. Finally, by (
S117
), (
S124
) and (
S125
), we conclude
h^{-1}n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{1}(V\mid\hat{\pi},\hat{\mu})-h_{1}(V\mid\bar{\pi},\bar{\mu})\}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(h^{-1}n^{-1/2})
.

S9.3
Bounding
\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{j}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{j}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}
for
j=2,3

When bounding
\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{2}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{2}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}
and
\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{3}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{3}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}
, the arguments will be similar. Therefore, we focus on bounding the former for brevity. Define,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\mu_{1}(X,m_{1})e_{u}(X)du.

We then write

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{2}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{2}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})-\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\}.

(S126)

By
Van der Vaart [2000, Theorem 19.14]
and Assumption
4
, we have the functional space
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}
which contains all possible functions
\hat{\mu}_{1}(x,m_{1}),\bar{\mu}_{1}(x,m_{1})
, is
P
-Donsker, because
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}
has a finite uniform entropy integral when
\delta=1
and a uniformly bounded (and thus also mean-squared-average bounded) envelop function; See the definition of
P
-Donsker in
Van der Vaart [2000,

§

19.2]
. Next, we consider the functional space,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mu)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})e_{u}(x)du\mid\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})\in\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}\Big\},

which contains functions for all possible
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})
.

Now we show that
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}})
is
P
-Donsker. Since
e_{u}(x)
is a fixed function and uniformly bounded (Assumptions
S10
and
S13
), we have
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e}=\{\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})e_{u}(x)\mid\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})\in\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}\}
is also
P
-Donsker by
Van der Vaart [2000, Theorem 19.14]
. Define
R(u)
as a fixed probability measure over
u
with probability density function
k_{u^{*}}(u)/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)du
and clearly
C_{\mathcal{E}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)du<+\infty
is a fixed constant under our regularity conditions. Then noticing that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})e_{u}(x)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\mathcal{E}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})e_{u}(x)R(du),

we can apply
Van der Vaart and van der Laan [2006, Lemma 5.2]
with
r=s=2
and deduce,

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mathcal{E}}\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}),\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}e}\|_{Q,2}/2,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\},

(S127)

where
G_{\mu_{1}e}(x,u)
can be any envelope function of
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e}
and, correspondingly, define

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{E}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\mathcal{E}}\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G^{2}_{\mu_{1}e}(x,u)dR(du)\Big\}^{1/2},

(S128)

which serves as an envelope function of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}})
by Hölder’s inequality.

On the other hand, noticing that for any
\nu(u,x)=\mu_{1}(x,m_{1})e_{u}(x)
,
\nu^{\prime}(v)=\mu_{1}^{\prime}(x,m_{1})e_{u}(x)
for any
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\nu-\nu^{\prime}\big\|_{Q,2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\{\sup_{x,u}e_{u}(x)\Big\}\cdot\big\|\mu_{1}(\cdot)-\mu^{\prime}_{1}(\cdot)\big\|_{Q,2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{e}\big\|\mu_{1}(\cdot)-\mu^{\prime}_{1}(\cdot)\big\|_{Q,2}

for some fixed constant
C_{e}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{x,u}e_{u}(x)\leq C_{e},

(S129)

as
e_{u}(x)
is uniformly bounded. Thus for any
(C_{e}^{-1}c)
-balls covering
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}
with functional centers,
\mu^{(1)}_{1}(x,m_{1}),\mu^{(2)}_{1}(x,m_{1})
, etc, the corresponding
c
-balls with functional centers,
e_{u}(x)\mu^{(1)}_{1}(x,m_{1}),

e_{u}(x)\mu^{(2)}_{1}(x,m_{1})
, etc, cover
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e}
for any
c>0
. Thus we have

\displaystyle N\{c,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle N\{C_{e}^{-1}c,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\},

(S130)

for any
c>0
and any random measure
Q
. Let
G_{\mu_{1}}(x,m_{1})
be an envelope function of
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}
satisfying Assumption
4
. By (
S129
), we can take
G_{\mu_{1}e}(x,u)=C_{e}G_{\mu_{1}}(x,m_{1})
as the envelop function of
\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e}
. Taking
c=\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}e}\|_{Q,2}/2=\epsilon C_{e}\|G_{\mu_{1}}\|_{Q,2}/2
in (
S130
) and considering the supremum over all possible
Q
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}e}\|_{Q,2}/2,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}e},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}}\|_{Q,2}/2,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}.

(S131)

Combing (
S127
) and (
S131
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mathcal{E}}\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}),\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}}\|_{Q,2}/2,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}

and therefore,

\displaystyle J\big\{1,\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}),\mathcal{L}_{2}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mathcal{E}}\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}}),\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}}d\epsilon

(S132)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}}\|_{Q,2}/2,\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}}d\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{1/2}\sqrt{\log N\{\epsilon\|G_{\mu_{1}}\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}},\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\}}d\epsilon

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle+\infty,

under Assumption
4
. In addition, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{G^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{C^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}C^{2}_{e}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G^{2}_{\mu_{1}}(m_{1},X)dR(du)\Big\}

(S133)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}C^{2}_{e}\Big\{\sup_{m_{1},x}G_{\mu_{1}}(m_{1},x)\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle+\infty,

as
G_{\mu_{1}}(m_{1},x)
is uniformly bounded. Summarizing (
S132
)–(
S133
) and by
Van der Vaart [2000, Theorem 19.14]
, we conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}})
is
P
-Donsker.

With the Donsker property, a standard application of the continuous mapping theorem
[
Van der Vaart, 2000
, Theorem 19.24]
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{n}\{\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})-\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(S134)

for any
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})
in
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\mu_{1}})
, as long as
\|\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})-\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\|_{\infty}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
; we omit this standard argument for simplicity and refer the interested reader to
Kennedy [2016,

§

4.2]
for details. Thus (
S134
) gives the desired bound for (
S126
). Therefore, to finish the proof, it is only left to show
\|\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})-\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\|_{\infty}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
which can be bounded as follows under Assumption
4
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{E}_{2}(\hat{\mu})-\mathcal{E}_{2}(\bar{\mu})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}k_{u^{*}}(u)\big\{\hat{\mu}_{1}(x,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{1}(x,m_{1})\big\}e_{u}(x)du\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mu}_{1}(\cdot,m_{1})-\bar{\mu}_{1}(\cdot,m_{1})\|_{\infty}C_{e}C_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Summarizing above results, we have
n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{2}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{2}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2})
, and symmetrically we can show
n^{-1/2}\mathbb{G}_{n}\{h_{3}(V\mid\hat{\mu})-h_{3}(V\mid\bar{\mu})\}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2})
. Combining the bounds in Sections
S9.2
and
S9.3
along with (
S118
), Lemma
S4
follows.

\Box

6.1

Figure S8
:
The box-plots of
\hat{\tau}_{u}-\tau^{*}_{u}
over
100
MC rounds when
n=500,1000
and

for the simulation in Section

. X-axis represents different data generation mechanisms. The top and bottom panels in each subfigure present the results when nuisance functions are estimated through parametric and semiparametric strategies, respectively.

We report the complete simulation results for our numerical experiments in Section
6.1
. In Figures
S8
(a)–(c), we present the histograms of
\hat{\tau}_{u}-\tau_{u}^{*}
over 100 Monte Carlo iterations under different data generation settings, with
n=500,1000
, and
2000
, respectively. In each subfigure, the top and bottom panels present the results when nuisance functions are estimated through parametric and semiparametric strategies, respectively. X-axis represents different data generation mechanisms. For example, (TP2,PS1,OM1) means data are generated from
(\mathcal{F}_{{\rm tp}},\mathcal{F}_{{\rm ps}},\mathcal{F}_{{\rm ps}})=(\mathcal{F}_{{\rm tp}}^{(2)},\mathcal{F}_{{\rm ps}}^{(1)},\mathcal{F}_{{\rm ps}}^{(1)})
. Different colors represent different principal levels
u
.

Under the data generation settings when
\hat{\tau}_{u}
can consistently estimate
\tau_{u}^{*}
as indicated by Theorem
6
, i.e., the data generation settings are (TP1,PS1,OM1), (TP2,PS1,OM1), (TP1,PS1, OM2) when nuisance functions are trained by the parametric strategy, and the data generation settings are (TP1,PS1,OM1), (TP2,PS1,OM1), (TP1,PS1,OM2), (TP2,PS1,OM2) when nuisance functions are trained by the semiparametric strategy, we can observe the corresponding estimation biases of
\hat{\tau}_{u}-\tau_{u}^{*}
are close to zero for all
n
, and in general, the estimation variances and biases become smaller as
n
increases, which verifies the nonparametric consistency of
\hat{\tau}_{u}
(cf. Theorem
6
). In addition, when all nuisance functions can be consistently esitmated, i.e., the data generation setting is (TP1,PS1,OM1) with parametric nuisance training and the data generation settings are (TP1,PS1,OM1), (TP2,PS1,OM1), (TP1,PS1,OM2), (TP2,PS1,OM2) with semiparametric nuisance training, the estimation errors of
\hat{\tau}_{u}
become particularly small due to the rate-double robustness (cf. Theorem
6
).
